To the Reader:

This report, prepared by the United States (U.S.) Department of Energy (DOE) West Valley Demonstration Project
(WVDP), represents a single, comprehensive summary of on-site and off-site environmental data collected during
calendar year 2024.

CH2M HILL BWXT West Valley, LLC (CHBWV) conducted Phase 1 Decommissioning activities at the WVDP since
2011 through CY 2025. In 2024, West Valley Cleanup Alliance, LLC (WVCA) was awarded the WVDP Phase 1B
Deactivation and Demolition (D&D) Contract by the DOE-EM and succeeded the WVDP decommissioning work
from CHBWV as of June 23, 2025.

Monitoring and surveillance of the WVDP facilities are conducted to verify that public health and safety and the
environment are protected. Environmental requirements and pollution prevention are integrated into work plan-
ning and execution. The quality assurance requirements applied to the environmental monitoring program by
CHBWYV and the DOE confirm the validity and accuracy of the monitoring data.

At the WVDP, radiological activities are conducted so that public exposure to ionizing radiation will be kept as low
as reasonably achievable and will not cause a total effective dose exceeding 100 mrem ina year, per DOE Order
458.1 Chg 4 “Radiation Protection of the Public and the Environment.” Radiological air emissions are controlled
and permitted by the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) under the National Emission Standards for
Hazardous Air Pollutants, Subpart H, regulations. Liquid effluent discharges are controlled and permitted through
the New York State Pollutant Discharge Elimination System (SPDES). Hazardous and mixed wastes are managed in
accordance with the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) interim status regulations and the New York
State Environmental Conservation Law.

Air, surface water, groundwater, stormwater, soil, sediment, and biological samples are collected and analyzed for
radiological and nonradiological constituents as part of a site-wide environmental monitoring program. The result-
ing data are evaluated to assess the effects of activities at the WVDP on the nearby public and the environment.

The dose to the critical receptor from airborne radiological emissions in 2024 was estimated to be 1.8% of the
10-millirem (mrem) EPA limit. The dose from combined airborne and waterborne radiological releases in 2024 was
estimated to be 0.18% of the 100-mrem DOE limit, verifying that the dose received by off-site residents continues
to be well below regulatory limits.

The WVDP employees achieved 707,503 consecutive safe work hours without a lost-time work injury or illness in
2024, while accomplishing complex decontamination, deconstruction, and waste management activities.

If you have any questions or comments about the information in this report, please contact WVDP
Communications at (716) 942-4996 or by e-mail at joseph. pillittere@wvdp.doe.gov. You are also encouraged to
complete and return the enclosed survey.

Sincerely,

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 ix

 x WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Purpose of This Report
The West Valley Demonstration Project (WVDP) is located on the site of the former and only commercial spent nuclear fuel
reprocessing facility to have operated in the United States. This former reprocessing facility was built in 1963 and was operated
by Nuclear Fuel Services, Inc. from 1966 to 1972. Since 1982, the site has been managed by the United States (U.S.) Department
of Energy (DOE) under the direction of the DOE’s Office of Environmental Management (DOE-EM). Although the spatial size of
the WVDP is not large (152 acres) compared with other nuclear cleanup sites, the WVDP operates under the same rigorous
level of DOE oversight as the larger sites, with site-specific procedural and regulatory requirements that align with its historical
background. The WVDP is located within the 3,338-acre New York State-owned Western New York Nuclear Service Center
(WNYNSC).

The Annual Site Environmental Report (ASER) for the WVDP describes the work performed each year and provides valuable
information about environmental conditions at the WVDP for members of the public, the DOE Headquarters (HQ), and other
interested stakeholders.

In accordance with DOE Order 231.1B Chg 1, “Environment, Safety, and Health Reporting,” this document summarizes calendar
year (CY) 2024 environmental monitoring data and describes the performance of the WVDP’s environmental management
system (EMS). The 2024 environmental monitoring data confirms that the WVDP activities remained in compliance with
environmental standards and regulations during 2024. WVDP activities are conducted by DOE in cooperation with the New
York State (NYS) Energy Research and Development Authority (NYSERDA).

Project Status
In 1980, Congress passed Public Law 96-368 (the WVDP Act), which is included in its entirety in Appendix H. The Act requires
the DOE to:

 1) Solidify the high-level radioactive waste (HLW) at the WVDP by vitrification into a form suitable for transportation and
 disposal.

 2) Develop containers suitable for the permanent disposal of the HLW.

 3) As soon as feasible, transport the solidified HLW to an appropriate federal repository for permanent disposal.

 4) Dispose of low-level radioactive waste (LLW) and transuranic (TRU) waste produced by the solidification of HLW at the
 WVDP.

 5) Decontaminate and decommission:

 a. tanks and other facilities where the HLW was stored;

 b. facilities used in the solidification of the waste, and;

 c. any material or hardware used in connection with the project.

As per the WVDP Act requirements, 1) and 2) were completed by September 2002. The remaining requirements of the WVDP
Act are or will be addressed consistent with the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) process.

Record of Decision (ROD). In April 2010, the DOE released a Record of Decision (ROD) for the Final Environmental Impact
Statement (Final EIS or FEIS) for the WVDP and the WNYNSC (“Final Environmental Impact Statement for Decommissioning
and/or Long-Term Stewardship at the West Valley Demonstration Project and Western New York Nuclear Service Center,” DOE/

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 EXE-1

Executive Summary

EIS-0226, issued on January 29, 2010). In the FEIS, the DOE and NYSERDA evaluated four alternatives: Site-wide Removal, Site-
wide Close-In-Place, Phased Decisionmaking, and No Action. Phased Decisionmaking was selected as the preferred alternative.
Under this alternative, decommissioning is being conducted in two phases. Phase 1 studies were performed to provide technical
evaluations that support the preparation of the Supplemental EIS (SEIS). These studies were completed in 2018.

These documents are found at https://www.energy.gov/wvdp/document-index.

Phase 1 Site Decommissioning involves the removal of a number of contaminated facilities, soil remediation, soil and facility
characterization, and focused studies that will facilitate future decisionmaking for the remaining facilities or areas on the prop-
erty.

Phase 2 will address the Waste Tank Farm (WTF), the waste disposal areas, the Construction and Demolition Debris Landfill
(CDDL), and the non-source area of the groundwater plume. The DOE intends to complete the remaining WVDP decisionmaking
with its Phase 2 decision in the SEIS. The SEIS will evaluate a range of alternatives including removal, in-place closure, and a
combination of those two.

Development of the Probabilistic Performance Assessment (PPA) and preparation of the draft SEIS continued in 2024. The DOE
and NYSERDA will use the PPA to evaluate the SEIS alternatives. The draft SEIS is expected to be published in 2025.

Contract Update
CH2M HILL BWXT West Valley, LLC (CHBWV) conducted Phase 1 Decommissioning activities at the WVDP from 2011 through
2024. In 2024, West Valley Cleanup Alliance, LLC (WVCA) was awarded the WVDP Phase 1B Deactivation and Demolition (D&D)
Contract by the DOE-EM and succeeded the WVDP decommissioning work from CHBWV as of June 23, 2025. This 2024 ASER
is published in October 2025 under the WVCA contract, however, this report uses CHBWV when referring to the site activities
performed in 2024.

2024 Accomplishments
In September 2022, the controlled deconstruction of the Main Plant Process Building (MPPB), a major project in Phase 1
decommissioning, began after years of thorough planning and deactivation activities. The deconstruction continued throughout
2024 while the on-site and off-site environment was monitored rigorously. As of the end of 2024, about 75% of planned
deconstruction work had been completed. The entire deconstruction was expected to take approximately 33 months. The
deconstruction completed on June 25, 2025.

The parts of the MPPB structure deconstructed in 2024 included: the Equipment Decontamination Room, the Chemical
Operating Aisle (COA), the majority of the Mechanical Operating Aisle (MOA), the Upper and Lower Extraction Aisles (UXA &
LXA), the Upper and Lower Warm Aisle (UWA & LWA), the Ventilation Wash Room (VWR), and the so-called “Green Giant,” the
sampler system inside the Chemical Process Cell (CPC.) For waste disposal, the remaining four High Integrity Containers (HICs)
and the last remaining five containers of legacy waste were shipped to the NNSS in 2024.

2024 Continued Projects
Permeable Treatment Wall (PTW) Performance. Overall, the PTW installed in November 2010 continues to mitigate the
groundwater plume, reducing the concentrations downgradient through 2024. Since 2021, elevated strontium-90 activity has
been detected in a few PTW wells and in the wells downgradient of the PTW. A study to identify methods to enhance the effec-
tiveness and increase the longevity of the PTW, which included the collection of soil, zeolite, and groundwater for laboratory
testing, was completed in 2024, and provided several recommendations, which are being evaluated for future implementation.

Tank 8D-4 (high-level waste storage tank) Sampling and Decommissioning. Tank liquid samples obtained in late 2023 were
shipped off site for radioisotope and general chemistry analyses in February 2024. Other progresses made in 2024 includes
video inspection of the tank interior, development and testing of a sludge sampling system, and obtaining sludge samples from
various locations within the tank.

EXE-2 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 Executive Summary

Compliance
The WVDP continued to operate in compliance with all applicable federal, state, and county environmental laws, regulations,
permits, and standards in 2024. (See Chapter 2, “Environmental Compliance Summary”.)

In 2024, there were:

 • No New York State Pollutant Discharge Elimination System (SPDES) permit effluent limit noncompliance events.

 • No exceedances of the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA’s) National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pol-
 lutants (NESHAP) dose standard.

 • No exceedances of the all pathway dose standard in DOE Order 458.1 Chg 4, “Radiation Protection of the Public and the
 Environment.”

In collaboration with the New York State Department of Environmental Conservation (NYSDEC), the WVDP continued to per-
form whole effluent toxicity (WET) testing on lagoon 3 discharges in 2024.

Worker Safety
As of December 31, 2024, CHBWV and its subcontractors achieved 707,503 consecutive work hours without lost-time work
because of accidents or illness. There were 0 hours of lost time attributed to injury or illness in CY 2024.

Environmental Management System (EMS)
Following the third-party on-site audit in April 2024, the CHBWV EMS was recommended for continued certification under the
EMS standard International Organization for Standardization (ISO) 14001:2015.

Environmental monitoring continued on site and off site in 2024 to detect changes in the environment resulting from the WVDP
project or historical site activities on the environment or human population. Airborne and waterborne effluents and biota were
sampled in accordance with the WVDP environmental monitoring program. There were no unplanned releases in 2024.

Preparation of closure certification reports for deconstructed structures for the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA)
continued in 2024. In 2024, certification reports for the Analytical & Process Chemistry (A&PC) and Lag Storage Area (LSA) #2
Hardstand were submitted to NYSDEC.

Environmental Monitoring
Airborne Radiological Monitoring Program. During 2024, monitoring of radiological emissions from on-site NESHAP-approved
emission points, including three active on-site building/facility stacks and up to 15 portable ventilation units (PVUs), continued.

Off-site ambient air monitoring continued at the 16 ambient air sampling stations that surround the WNYNSC premises. The
ambient air monitors were in operation 98.7% of the time in 2024. Measurement results demonstrated that airborne releases
to the environment were well within permissible limit (Table C-6).

Surface Water Radiological Releases Monitoring Program. Waterborne radiological releases from the site were measured at
two natural streams, sampled at locations WNSWAMP and WNSW74A, and one controlled outfall discharging from lagoon 3,
sampled at outfall 001 (location code WNSP001) (Figure 4-4 in Chapter 4). Off-site surface water was sampled at two down-
stream locations, WFFELBR and WFBCTB. (See Figure 4-8.) All measurements demonstrated that waterborne releases to the
environment were within permissible limits.

Groundwater Monitoring Program. Radiological isotopes and nonradiological chemical parameters and water levels were rou-
tinely monitored at 64 wells and seepage locations across the WVDP site. Two metals, chromium and nickel, have been detected
above their NYS Technical Standards at three wells; the source of these detections is believed to be corrosion of the stainless steel
well screens. Tributyl phosphate (TBP) was detected at one monitoring well, but the concentration of this chemical has been

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 EXE-3

Executive Summary

decreasing steadily over time. Elevated levels of strontium-90 are present within the well-defined groundwater plume on the
north plateau and are also observed in some isolated areas within and downgradient of the PTW, which continues to be moni-
tored closely. The sampling results of emerging contaminants identified by the EPA (organic chemicals such as 1,4-dioxane and
1,2,3-trichloropropane) in groundwater were nondetects.

Drinking Water Monitoring Program. In accordance with the Cattaraugus County Health Department (CCHD) requirements,
sampling for per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) and 1,4-dioxane was performed on the drinking water supply wells in
2024, and all results were less than the analytical detection limits. According to the EPA’s final rule on PFAS limits, issued in April
2024, and the ASER Guideline, the PFAS sampling reporting format has been updated in this 2024 ASER. Tables B-5I and B-5J
now include six PFAS species and the EPA Hazard Index.

Radiological Dose Assessment
Airborne dose. The estimated dose in 2024 from airborne emissions from the WVDP activities was determined based on the
data from the ambient air samplers. The total estimated annual dose from airborne emissions from point sources and ambient
air in 2024 was 0.18 millirem (mrem) (0.0018 millisievert [mSv]). Compliance with the airborne dose regulations was demon-
strated based on the annual average radioisotopic concentrations at the 16 off-site air samplers, where the averages were all
well below the 10-mrem/yr (0.1 mSv) limit established by the EPA.

Waterborne dose. The estimated dose in 2024 from waterborne sources was 0.004 mrem (0.00004 mSv) based on measure-
ments of the radioactivity in discharges from the site.

The total estimated maximum potential dose based on emissions from both airborne and waterborne sources in 2024 was
0.184 mrem (0.00184 mSv), which is well below the annual 100-mrem limit established by DOE Order 458.1. (See Table 5-2.) In
comparison, the average dose to a member of the public in the U.S. from natural background and artificial sources is 620 mrem
per year (National Council on Radiation Protection and Measurements [NCRP] Report No. 160, 2009).

Dose to Biota. Radioisotope concentrations in milk and venison (white-tailed deer) samples collected near the site were indis-
tinguishable from concentrations of background (control) samples. The dose to biota evaluation for 2024 concluded that aquat-
ic and terrestrial biota populations (both plants and animals) were not exposed to doses above the DOE biota dose standard of
1 rad/day (radiation absorbed dose) for aquatic animals and terrestrial plants, and 0.1 rad/day for riparian and terrestrial ani-
mals.

Quality Assurance (QA)
The data presented in this report are validated in accordance with WVDP QA procedures. The WVDP QA program includes
evaluations of the performance of subcontract laboratories and routine assessments of the environmental and regulatory com-
pliance programs. Subcontract laboratories that analyze WVDP environmental samples participated in independent radiological
and nonradiological constituent performance evaluation studies. In these studies, environmental test samples with concentra-
tions only known by the testing agency were analyzed by the laboratories. Of 195 performance evaluation analyses conducted
for the WVDP, 96.9% were within accepted limits.

Conclusion
The WVDP complied with all environmental regulations and DOE directives and the project was operated in a safe manner
throughout CY 2024. The environmental data collected and evaluated in 2024 indicate that the WVDP conducted the MPPB
deconstruction and other site activities protecting site workers, the public and the environment, while preparing for the next
phase of decommissioning.

EXE-4 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 CHAPTER 1
 INTRODUCTION
Site History
The West Valley Demonstration Project (WVDP) is a U.S. Department of Energy (DOE)-operated cleanup site located within the
New York State-owned Western New York Nuclear Service Center (WNYNSC) where commercial spent nuclear fuel reprocessing
operations were previously conducted in the late 1960s and early 1970s.

The scope of the DOE’s mission at the WVDP is established by the WVDP Act
of 1980 (PL 98-356) (Appendix H), signed by President Jimmy Carter. The Act
includes solidification of liquid high-level radioactive waste (HLW) that was
generated from the prior commercial spent nuclear fuel (SNF) reprocessing
operations, decontamination and decommissioning of facilities used in the
solidification process, and disposal of waste generated. HLW solidification ac-
tivities were completed in 2002, and in more recent years, significant facility
decontamination and removal actions have been completed, as well as off-site
shipment and disposal of low-level radioactive waste (LLW). President Carter's remarks at the signing ceremony can
 be read at https://shorturl.at/no19D
The activities at the site in 2024 were primarily focused on the deconstruction
of the building that had housed the former SNF reprocessing operations [the Main Plant Process Building (MPPB)], as well as
waste packaging, storage, and off-site disposal. The deconstruction of the MPPB aboveground structure is completed in June
2025. For a more detailed site history timeline, refer to Table H-1 in the Appendix H, "West Valley Demonstration Project Act
and the Timeline of the WVNSC and WVDP" of this report.

Site Location and General Environmental Setting
The WVDP is located in western New York State (NYS), about 30 miles (mi) (50 kilometers [km]) south of Buffalo, New York. The
WVDP facilities currently occupy a security-fenced area of about 152 acres (61 hectares [ha]) within the 3,338-acre (1,351 ha)
WNYNSC premises located in the Town of Ashford in northern Cattaraugus County. An aerial photo of the WVDP is presented
in Figure 1-2.

Climate. Although extremes of 99oF (37oC) and -20oF (-29oC) have been recorded in western New York (NY) (weather station:
Buffalo, NY), the climate is moderate, with an average annual temperature of 48.1oF (8.9oC). (Average data for 1940 to 2024 from
The National Weather Service, National Oceanic, and Atmospheric Administration [NOAA], https://www.ncdc.noaa.gov/cag).
Precipitation is markedly influenced by Lake Erie to the west and, to a lesser extent, by Lake Ontario to the north.

Based on data collected at the on-site meteorological tower, the recent 10-year average annual precipitation at the WVDP was
43.54 inches/year (110.59 cm). Total precipitation in 2024 was 43.96 inches (111.73 cm), very close to the 10-year average.
Predominant wind directions are generally from the northwest and southeast that follow valleys extending toward Lake Erie,
located north-northwest of the WVDP. The on-site meteorological tower continuously monitors the site-specific wind speed,
wind direction, temperature, and barometric pressure. This data is accessible in real-time to site operations personnel.

Ecology. The WNYNSC lies within the northern deciduous forest biome, and the diversity of its vegetation is typical of the re-
gion. Equally divided between forest and open land, the WNYNSC premises provides a habitat attractive to white-tailed deer
and various indigenous and migratory birds, reptiles, and small mammals. No species on the federal endangered species list are
known to reside on the WNYNSC premises.

Geology and Hydrology. The WVDP premises lies on the Allegheny Plateau in New York State (NYS) at an elevation of approx-
imately 1,300 to 1,450 feet (ft) (approximately 400 to 440 meters [m]) above mean sea level (MSL). The underlying geology

 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 1-3

(continued from page 1-1)

includes a sequence of glacial sediments above shale bedrock. The WVDP site is drained by three small streams (Franks Creek,
Quarry Creek, and Erdman Brook) and is divided by a stream valley (Erdman Brook) into two general areas: the north plateau
and the south plateau.

Franks Creek, which receives drainage from Erdman Brook and Quarry Creek, flows into Buttermilk Creek, which enters
Cattaraugus Creek and flows westward away from the WNYNSC premises. (See Figures A-1 and A-5 in Appendix A.) Cattaraugus
Creek ultimately drains into Lake Erie to the northwest.

Relevant Demographics
Although several roads and a railway approach or pass through the WNYNSC, the public is prohibited from accessing the
WNYNSC. A public deer hunting program on the WNYNSC premises managed by New York State Energy Research and Develop-
ment Authority (NYSERDA) was suspended in 2022. No unescorted public access is allowed on the WVDP site.

Land near the WNYNSC is used primarily for agriculture and arboriculture. Cattaraugus Creek downstream of the WNYNSC is
used locally for fishing and for recreational activities (such as swimming and kayaking). Although some water is taken from the
creek to irrigate nearby golf course greens and tree farms, no public drinking water is drawn from the creek before it flows into
Lake Erie. Water from Lake Erie is used as a public drinking water supply. Industrial water and potable water used for the WVDP
activities are supplied by bedrock groundwater wells.

The communities of West Valley, Riceville, the town of Ashford, and the Village of Springville are located within approximately
5 miles (8 km) of the WVDP. The population around the site is sparse with an average population density of Cattaraugus County
of about 58 persons/mi2 (23 persons/km2) (United States [U.S.] Census 2020). No major industries are located within this area.

Decommissioning Progress
The WVDP decommissioning consists of two major steps, Phase 1 and Phase 2. In January 2010, the DOE and NYSERDA issued
the “Final Environmental Impact Statement (FEIS) for Decommissioning and/or Long-Term Stewardship at the West Valley
Demonstration Project and Western New York Nuclear Service Center,” DOE/EIS-0226. In the FEIS, the DOE and NYSERDA
evaluated four alternatives: Site-wide Removal, Site-wide Close-In-Place, Phased Decisionmaking, and No Action. Phased
Decisionmaking was identified as the preferred alternative. Under this alternative, it was decided that the decommissioning
would be conducted in two phases. The work performed in 2024 to remove the above-grade portion of the MPPB was part of
the Phase 1 decommissioning scope.

The DOE and NYSERDA are jointly preparing a Supplemental EIS (the SEIS) to implement the next phase (Phase 2) of decommis-
sioning. The Proposed Action of the SEIS will address the decontamination and decommissioning of the facilities remaining at
the WVDP site after the completion of Phase 1 decommissioning in accordance with the 2010 final EIS (FEIS) Record of Decision
(ROD). Figure 1-3 on page 1-6 shows the timeline of decommissioning, SEIS, and major projects under each phase.

The SEIS is being prepared pursuant to the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) and the New York State Environmental
Quality Review Act (SEQR). Phase 2 decisions will be informed by Phase 1 and other scientific studies being performed at the
WVDP site. An explanation of Phase 1 and Phase 2 decommissioning work scope and a more detailed progress update for each
of the SEIS study areas is provided in Chapter 2, "Environmental Compliance Summary" of this report.

Work on the long-term probabilistic performance assessment (PPA) for the WVDP site continued in 2024. The PPA simulates the
outcomes of decommissioning and site end-stage alternatives, which are described in the FEIS, using computer modeling. The
scenarios for simulation include HLW tank decommissioning options, erosion and natural disaster risk assessment, and a long-
term health risk assessment. The simulation results will be evaluated in the SEIS.

The draft SEIS, including the DOE and NYSERDA preferred decommissioning alternative, is expected to be published in 2027. A
six-month period for public comment on the SEIS will follow the issuance of the draft report. A final SEIS will subsequently be
issued, followed by the Phase 2 decommissioning Record of Decision (ROD) and Findings Statement.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 1-5

By the end of 2030, the DOE expects to complete the decommissioning of the below-grade portions of the MPPB, as well as the
Vitrification Facility (VF), where aboveground deconstruction was completed in 2019. The DOE will also complete the decom-
missioning of the site’s radioactive water treatment system, including four active lagoons and one closed lagoon. By the end of
2032, the DOE expects to complete soil remediation efforts in the Waste Management Area (WMA)-1 and WMA-2. (See Figures
A-9 and A-10 for the WMA boundaries.)

2024 Site Accomplishments
The WVDP continued to execute Phase 1 decommissioning work scope throughout 2024. The MPPB deconstruction project,
which began in September 2022, continued to be a major focus of work activity during 2024.

As of the end of 2024, approximately 75% of planned deconstruction work had been safely completed. An animation of the
planned deconstruction sequence can be viewed on YouTube (https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=kobznp07Mow). The time-
line of the MPPB deconstruction with major events can be seen on the previous page. At the end of 2024, the site landscape had
been drastically changed as compared to 2022, prior to the initiation of the above-grade removal efforts .

 (SSC) using a small elevator and conveyor inside the wall.

Radiological Waste Disposition
The WVDP utilized three primary disposal facilities for the site's radiological waste disposition in 2024: Energy Solutions in Utah,
Waste Control Specialists (WCS) in Texas, and the Nevada Nuclear Security Site (NNSS) in Nevada.

  • High Integrity Containers (HICs). Eight HICs contained highly radioactive resin-like material used in the filters in the Fuel
 Receiving and Storage (FRS) building pool that was previously used to store the spent fuel rods during the original Nuclear
 Fuel Services, Inc. (NFS) project in the late 1960s and early 1970s. The four HICs were shipped off site in 2023 and the
 remaining four HICs were shipped to the WCS in 2024.

  • CPC Degraded Containers. The planning and preparation for the disposition of the highly radioactive CPC Degraded
 Containers began in 2022 and continued in 2024. Progress during the year included transfer to the Remote Handled Waste
 Facility (RHWF) to support readiness for packaging, and the fabrication of specialty containers to be used to support
 transportation and off-site disposal. Shipment for the remaining two containers is planned for 2025.

  • Legacy Oversized Containers. Shipment of legacy oversized waste began in 2022 and continued in 2024.; two of them were
 pumps used at the WTF. The last remaining five containers were shipped to the NNSS in March 2025.

Other Continued Projects in 2024
  • Former HLW 8D-4 Tank Sampling. Significant milestones in tank liquid and sludge characterization were achieved in 2024.
 Tank liquid samples obtained in late 2023 were shipped off site for radioisotope and general chemistry analyses in February
 2024. Steps taken to verify the liquid level of the 8D-4 tank and an interior inspection were completed in February 2024.
 Characterization of 8D-4 tank contents will continue in 2025. For more details, see Chapter 2, "Environmental Compliance
 Summary."
  • Permeable Treatment Wall (PTW) Performance Evaluation. The discussions to evaluate PTW performance continued in
 2024. Based on the samples collected in 2023, several options to extend the longevity of the wall (installed in 2010), were
 presented to the DOE and NYSERDA. Currently, the DOE and NYSERDA are evaluating these options.
  • Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) Closures. The demolition of the A&PC Hot Cells was completed and the
 final RCRA Hazardous Waste Closure Certification Report for A&PC was submitted. The Hazardous Waste Closure Certifica-
 tion report was submitted for Lag Storage Area (LSA) #2 Hardstand.

 CHAPTER 2
 ENVIRONMENTAL COMPLIANCE SUMMARY

Water Releases: All State Pollutant Discharge Elimination System (SPDES) discharges were within applicable SPDES permit limits
in 2024. The results of the SPDES whole effluent toxicity (WET) tests performed during 2024 were compliant with SPDES permit
limits.

Waste Management: In 2024, 678 boxes, 12,451 tons of wastes (including general debris and specialty wastes), were shipped
off site. As of the end of 2024, about 90% of the wastes expected to be generated during the MPPB deconstruction were boxed,
shipped and received by the disposal facility.

Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) Closure Activities: Quarterly Progress Reports for the RCRA §3008(h)
Administrative Order on Consent and Quarterly Exception Reports for RCRA Groundwater Monitoring Results were submitted
to NYDEC on schedule. The RCRA Closure Certification Reports for the LSA #2 Hardstand and the A&PC Hot Cells were submitted
in 2024 and accepted by NYSDEC in early 2025 to document clean closure of these units.

National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA): SEIS scoping and the preparation of draft SEIS and the PPA modeling continued in
2024. The draft SEIS is expected to be issued in 2027, and public hearings will follow.

Radiation Protection of the Public and the Environment (DOE Order 458.1 Chg 4): The dose estimated from 2024 air emissions
and water discharges containing radionuclides was well below the DOE public dose limit of 100 millirem (mrem) per year from
all pathways.

Air Emissions: The off-site ambient air sampling data indicated that the estimated dose based on air emissions in 2024 was well
below the 10 mrem per year EPA compliance limit.

Compliance Program at the WVDP
This chapter summarizes the laws, regulations, permits, and agreements pertaining to the WVDP activities, environmental
protection, and the public and worker, and the WVDP's compliance status in 2024 with regard to

 • Federal environmental protection statutes and associated state and local environmental regulations;

 • Permits required under specific environmental protection regulations; and

 • EPA or NYSDEC issuance of notices of violation or noncompliance.

The EPA, NYSDEC, and the DOE have established standards for radiological and nonradiological effluents to protect human
health and the environment. The DOE applies to the EPA for approval to release limited amounts of radiological constituents
into the air and applies to the NYSDEC for permits to release limited amounts of non-radiological constituents into the air and
water. The permits issued by these agencies describe release points, management and reporting requirements, and discharge
limits on pollutants and define the sampling and analysis regimen. The following sections report major compliance drivers
applicable to the WVDP activities and compliance status updates for the drivers. The environmental permits, approvals, and
registrations applicable to the WVDP are listed in Table 2-9, and the applicable federal and state regulations are listed in Table
2-10 at the end of this chapter. Environmental monitoring results are presented in Chapter 4, "Environmental Monitoring."

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 2-1

Radiological Air Emissions Compliance
The Clean Air Act (CAA), administered through the EPA, requires compliance with the National Emission Standards for Hazardous
Air Pollutants (NESHAP) under Title 40 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR) Part 61, Subpart H. Sources of radioactive discharges
to the atmosphere are regulated directly by the EPA. In New York State (NYS), the EPA has delegated to NYSDEC the authority
to regulate nonradiological emissions to the atmosphere.

The NESHAP standard states that no member of the public may receive an Effective Dose Equivalent (EDE) greater than 10 mrem
(0.1 millisievert [mSv]) per year resulting from radionuclide emissions to the atmosphere. The EPA approval must be obtained
before conducting any work where it is expected that the potential to emit in dose will be 1% (0.1 mrem/year) or more of the
10 mrem standard.

Open-Air Deconstruction Using an EPA-Approved Alternative Method
NESHAP compliance is demonstrated at the WVDP based on the data collected at the ambient air samplers during the
year. NESHAP regulations allow two methods for demonstrating compliance, (1) the “measure and model” approach
which involves measuring radiological emissions in air released from point sources (such as stack effluents) and using EPA-
approved computer models to estimate dose to the maximally exposed off-site individual (MEOSI), or (2) the “environ-
mental measurement” approach which involves measuring environmental concentrations (ambient monitoring) of air-
borne radionuclides at ambient air monitoring locations and evaluating dose at the critical receptor. Since 2014, with EPA
approval, NESHAP compliance at the WVDP has been demonstrated using the “environmental measurement” approach. This
method is more appropriate since the majority of potential emissions from the WVDP have shifted from point sources to
diffuse sources as more facilities are decommissioned or deconstructed.

An alternative methodology for estimating radionuclide emissions to air was developed by the DOE-WVDP and its subcon-
tractors and was approved by the EPA in May 2016 for use to predict emissions during VF deconstruction. Validation involved
collecting actual emissions data during VF deconstruction and comparing it to the emissions that were predicted. This compar-
ison, “the validation study,” was performed during the deconstruction of the VF from September 2017 to September 2018. As
a result of the study, the WVDP modified the emission factors in the Alternative Methodology to improve the accuracy of the
analysis. In July 2019, the EPA approved the use of the revised radiological source term estimation methodology to demonstrate
compliance with 40 CFR Part 61 Subpart H for other facilities undergoing deconstruction. The WVDP used this methodology to
estimate airborne emissions predicted for the deconstruction of the MPPB.

In the fall of 2012, an ambient air monitoring network was installed surrounding the WVDP, consisting of 16 low-volume sam-
pling stations (one for each of the 16 compass sectors) and one high-volume sampler (which can measure lower concentrations)
in the sector most often identified as having the maximum estimated dose. The WVDP monitors the operational status of the
monitors according to the NESHAP permit, and the annual percentage of operation in 2024 was 98.7%.

Routine ambient air network sampling results are discussed in Chapter 4, "Environmental Monitoring" and are tabulated in
Appendix C. These data are used to estimate the annual dose from air emissions for NESHAP compliance as described in
Chapter 5, "Dose Assessment."

2-2 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

Nonradiological Releases

The WVDP currently has an Air Facility Registration Certificate for nonradiological emission sources as required by NYSDEC and
per 6NYCRR Chapter III, Part 201, but does not have any nonradiological sources that require air emissions permits. All potential
nonradiological point sources of air emissions are evaluated annually against permitting requirements, and the results in 2024
were determined to be exempt or below permitting release limits.

Asbestos releases are regulated separately from other nonradiological releases under NESHAP and NYSDOL regulations (12
NYCRR Part 56). The asbestos NESHAP and NYSDOL regulations require notifications to the EPA and NYS, respectively, before
deconstruction of any structure that could contain asbestos or asbestos-containing material (ACM) in an amount exceeding
regulatory thresholds to ensure the safety of the workers and the public. NYS also requires notification of any activities involv-
ing the removal or abatement of any asbestos or ACM in amounts exceeding the regulatory thresholds. Table 2-3 provides a
summary of the asbestos quantities managed in 2024. In 2024, two projects involving ACM were performed: the continued
deconstruction of the MPPB and the deconstruction of the two locker room trailers.

Air Quality Compliance Update for 2024

All airborne releases of radiological constituents from the WVDP in 2024 were within the permissible EPA limits and DOE guid-
ance. The estimated maximum potential dose to any off-site resident from air emissions in 2024 was significantly less than the
10 mrem NESHAP compliance limit as determined by the data collected at the ambient air samplers. (See Chapter 5, "Dose
Assessment" for more discussion.) There were no releases that were out of compliance with EPA and NYSDEC regulations as
summarized in Table 2-1.

Water Quality Compliance

WVDP State Pollutant Discharge Elimination System (SPDES) Permit
The Clean Water Act (CWA), administered in NYS by NYSDEC through EPA delegated authority, requires that all process water
discharges and stormwater discharges from the site be compliant with the WVDP SPDES permit. The current site permit was is-
sued in 2011 and regulates nonradiological liquid discharges through the site’s monitored wastewater treatment system outfall
and stormwater outfalls. With NYSDEC's approval, the WVDP is currently continuing to operate under the 2011 SPDES permit.
The SPDES Permit is issued in compliance with Title 8 of Article 17 of the NYS Environmental Conservation Law (ECL), with the
CWA.

SPDES and Stormwater Update for 2024
All SPDES discharges were within applicable SPDES permit limits as shown by Table 2-2 on the previous page. Stormwater was
monitored semiannually as required by the SPDES permit. No SPDES exceedances of stormwater compliance limits occurred in
2024.

The SPDES permit requires WET testing every five years, and the last testing was performed on four discharges spanning from
2022 to 2023. The WVDP, upon request of NYSDEC, performed supplemental WET testing twice and Toxic Inventory/Reduction
Evaluations (TI/RE) in 2024 to evaluate sporadically observed toxicity. WET sampling was performed at the outfall 001 in accor-
dance with the SPDES permit, and the sampling results were compliant with the action levels. In 2024, only invertebrate (water
flea) species tests were conducted, with the agreement with NYSDEC. (See Table B-2K.) The WET test will continued to be per-
formed in 2025 at the time of outfall 001 discharges.

The annual review of the WVDP CWA/SPDES best management practices and stormwater pollution prevention plan (SWPPP)
was completed in November 2024. It was determined that no plan revisions were necessary in 2024.

Surface Water Radiological Release Compliance
Releases of radiological constituents in water effluents are subject to the requirements in DOE Order 458.1 (“Radiation Pro-
tection of the Public and the Environment”) and DOE-STD-1196-2022 (“Derived Concentration Technical Standard”). DOE
Order 458.1 requires environmental monitoring of the air, water, groundwater and biota in order to ensure that the maximum
potential public radiation dose from all pathways remains under 100 mrem/year. DOE-STD-1196-2022 establishes Derived Con-
centration Standards (DCSs) to be used in the design and conduct of radiological environmental programs at DOE facilities. (See
the inset box, "Radiological Data Evaluation" in Chapter 4, "Environmental Monitoring" for additional information on DCSs.)

Compliance with DOE Order 458.1 for process water and non-process waterborne releases to the environment is based on dose
and is discussed in Chapter 5, "Dose Assessment."

Radiological Release Update for 2024
All waterborne releases of radiological constituents from the WVDP in 2024 (from the SPDES outfall 001 [location code
WNSP001] and natural surface water effluents such as WNSWAMP and WNSW74A) were within permissible limits. The total
estimated dose from the waterborne release pathway was well below the DOE 100 mrem/year limit for all pathways.

Water Withdrawal and Drinking Water Compliance
New York State, as one of the participating states in the Great Lakes - St. Lawrence River Basin Water Resources Compact
Council, regulates water withdrawal systems having the capacity to withdraw 100,000 gallons per day (gpd) or more. NYSDEC
manages this water withdrawal and reporting program under NYS ECL Article 15, Title 5, and NYCRR 601.5. The WVDP reports
the amount of groundwater and reservoir water withdrawn annually to the NYSDEC pursuant to the site water withdrawal per-
mit issued in December 2019.

Potable and industrial water has been supplied by two groundwater wells located on the site since 2014. Prior to this, water was
supplied by two reservoirs immediately south of the site. The reservoirs currently provide water for fire protection and SPDES
discharge flow augmentation. The Cattaraugus County Health Department (CCHD) has assigned the WVDP drinking water (pota-
ble water) system a NYS tracking number and determines the required sampling for the WVDP drinking water system annually.

In November 2024, the WVDP conducted a lead service line survey on the potable (drinking) water system at the site in accor-
dance with the EPA requirements under 40 CFR Part 141 Subpart I [141.48(a)] and NY State Department of Health requirements
under 10 NYCRR 5-1.42. Written records and field verification determined that there were no lead service lines in the potable
water system at the WVDP.

Analytical results of the well water and potable water at the site are presented in Appendix B-5.

2-4 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

Water Withdrawal Update for 2024
The 2024 WVDP water withdrawal report was submitted to NYSDEC in March 2025. In 2024, controlled discharges from lagoon
3 were performed in February, May, and November, with a total volume of 5,827,550 gallons discharged. The maximum daily
withdrawal volume in 2024 was 323,564 gallons during the February discharge. The average daily withdrawal rate in 2024
was 217,852 gallons.

The total amount of augmentation water used during these discharges totaled 11,081,400 gallons (41,947,662 liters).
Augmentation water is used during lagoon 3 discharges to reduce total dissolved solids (TDS) concentrations.

Waste Management Compliance
The DOE sites that manage radioactive waste are required to develop, document, implement, and maintain a site-wide radioac-
tive waste management program under DOE Order 435.1 Chg 2, Radioactive Waste Management. The order aims to ensure that
all radioactive waste generated at DOE sites is managed in a manner that protects workers and public health.

At the WVDP, waste is managed in accordance with 1) "Waste Acceptance Manual" for HLW, 2) "TRU Waste Management Pro-
gram Plan" for TRU waste, 3) "LLW Management Program Plan" for LLW, and 4) "Site Treatment Plan (STP)" for the radioactive
component of mixed LLW.

Waste Treatment, Storage, and Disposal. Waste generated at the WVDP is shipped to off-site waste storage and disposal facil-
ities. The DOE Consolidated Audit Program (DOECAP), under DOE Order 414.1D Chg 2, "Quality Assurance, " ensures that con-
tracted commercial treatment, storage, and disposal facilities (TSDFs) are audited on their ability to provide services in a man-
ner that is technically defensible and legally compliant. The WVDP conducts the QA of contracted analytical laboratories and
waste storage and disposal facilities annually and submits an annual TSDF report to DOE. The WVDP QA program is described
in Chapter 7, "Quality Assurance." The RCRA section below describes the waste management compliance and activities at the
WVDP in detail.

Nonradioactive, Nonhazardous, Regulated Waste Management (Non-RCRA). Nonradioactive, nonhazardous waste is also
shipped off site to solid waste management facilities. Sanitary wastewaters are shipped to the Buffalo Sewer Authority, the
Gowanda Sewage Treatment Plant, or the Arcade Sewage Treatment Plant for treatment and disposal. This waste volume is
provided in the annual water withdrawal report.

 Overpacked Chemical Process Cell specialty waste container ready for shipment off site

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 2-5

 RCRA Permit and §3008(h) Consent Order History at the WVDP
 Hazardous Waste Permitting - RCRA Interim Status Permit Application. In 1984, the DOE notified the EPA of hazardous
 waste activities at the WVDP and identified DOE-WVDP as a hazardous waste generator. In 1990, to comply with Title 6
 New York State Official Compilation of Codes, Rules, and Regulations (6 NYCRR) Part 373-3, a RCRA Part A (i.e., Interim
 Status or Part A) Permit Application for the WVDP was filed with NYSDEC for activities associated with storage and treat-
 ment of hazardous waste. The WVDP has operated under interim status ever since. RCRA facility operations are limited to
 those described in the Part A Permit Application and must comply with an interim status regulations unless certain exemp-
 tions apply. The RCRA Part A Permit Application must be revised prior to changes to the WVDP’s RCRA waste management
 operations.

 According to Part A requirements, the DOE prepares closure plans for the hazardous waste management units at the WVDP.
 The closure plans are transmitted to NYSDEC for approval in anticipation of closure activities and are revised to address
 NYSDEC comments or changes in activities. To complete the closure of a RCRA unit, all wastes are removed, and other
 actions, such as decontamination or removal of structures, are taken to meet the specific RCRA closure requirements. A
 closure certification report is then prepared for approval by NYSDEC to document closure was performed in accordance with
 the NYSDEC-approved RCRA closure plan.

 RCRA Final Status Permit Application. In 2003, NYSDEC requested the submittal of a 6 NYCRR Part 373-2 Permit Application
 (i.e., Final Status or Part B) for the WVDP, which was transmitted to NYSDEC in December 2004. On April 16, 2009,
 NYSDEC requested the submittal of a revised Part B Permit Application for the WVDP, which was submitted to NYSDEC on
 September 30, 2010. On March 22, 2012, NYSDEC notified NYSERDA and the DOE that they had reassessed the WVDP RCRA
 regulatory program and that the processing of the September 30, 2010, Part B permit application, including revisions, would
 be deferred. As a result, the WVDP continues to operate as an interim status facility pursuant to its Part A Permit application.

 RCRA §3008(h) Consent Order (Docket No. II RCRA §3008(h) 92-0202) (abbreviated as "Consent Order"). Section §3008(h)
 of RCRA authorizes the EPA to issue an order requiring corrective action at RCRA Part A Interim Status facilities to protect
 human health and the environment from a release of hazardous waste or hazardous constituents to the environment from a
 Solid Waste Management Unit (SWMU). (See Table 2-10 on Page 2-26.) The DOE and NYSERDA entered into a Consent Order
 with the EPA and NYSDEC in March 1992. Consent Order activities performed at the WVDP to date include the following:

 • The RCRA Facility Investigation (RFI) evaluated potential releases of RCRA-regulated hazardous constituents from
 SWMUs. Final RFI reports were submitted in 1997, with no corrective actions required, with the exception of
 groundwater monitoring as outlined in the RFI and approved by the EPA and NYSDEC.

 • The Solid Waste Management Unit (SWMU) and Super SWMU (SSWMU) Assessment and Current Conditions
 Report. SSWMU refers to a unit where two or more SWMUs are treated as one larger waste management unit.
 These reports, initially submitted in 2004 and most recently updated in 2020, summarized the historical activities at
 each SWMU, providing environmental monitoring data and an update on activities performed since the RFI reports
 were submitted. See Table 2-7 for a complete list of WVDP RCRA SSWMUs.

 • As a result of reviewing the current conditions report in 2004, NYSDEC requested additional evaluations for
 six SWMUs (the NDA Burial Area, NDA Interceptor Trench, Demineralizer Sludge Ponds, lagoon 1, the CDDL, and the
 LLWTF) as Corrective Measures Studies (CMSs). These studies, submitted to NYSDEC and the EPA in 2010, identified
 and evaluated potential corrective measures and made recommendations on remedial alternatives.

 • The 1990 and 2008 Interim Measures (IMs) for the NDA were implemented, (1) to intercept and collect groundwa-
 ter within the NDA potentially contaminated with a mixture of n-dodecane and tributyl phosphate (TBP), and (2) to
 minimize water infiltration into the NDA and groundwater flow through the NDA, thereby minimizing the potential
 release of impacted groundwater until the final disposition of the NDA is determined. Liquid organic material was
 never observed. Therefore, with NYSDEC and EPA approval, in 2019, the Liquid Pretreatment System (LPS) building
 was removed, and a geomembrane cover was placed over the building footprint.

Waste Generation and Storage Activity Update for 2024
Most of the construction and demolition (C&D) waste generated during FY 2024 resulted from waste generation associated
with the demolition of the MPPB. C&D debris from this project could not be diverted or recycled primarily due to the presence
of radiological and/or asbestos contamination. The total amount of C&D waste, including radiological, asbestos, or hazardous
wastes sent to landfills, was approximately 28,478 m³ (31,392.5 tons). This was a 19% increase in the generation of C&D waste
as compared to FY 2023.

 • At the end of FY 2024, a total of 86,776 lbs of mixed and hazardous waste were stored on the WVDP site (85,900 lbs
 at the end of 2023).

 • Zero TRU waste was generated in 2024. As of the end of 2024, a total of 53.84 m3 of mixed TRU waste (liquid and solid)
 was stored as residual waste in tank 8D-4. The volume slightly decreased from the quantity in December 2024 (54.44
 m3).

 • The Annual Hazardous Waste Report for 2024 waste activities was submitted to NYSDEC in February 2025. The reported
 quantities generated, treated, and shipped are shown in Table 2-3.

A summary of non-RCRA regulated waste as required to be reported in the ASER in compliance with DOE Order 435.1,
“Radioactive Waste Management,” is listed in Table 2-3.
Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA)
RCRA and its implementing regulations govern the life cycle of hazardous waste from “cradle-to-grave” and mandate that gen-
erators take responsibility for ensuring the proper treatment, storage, and ultimate disposal of their wastes. A hazardous waste
permit is required for facilities that store large quantities of hazardous waste for more than 90 days or treat and/or dispose of
hazardous waste at the facility.

The EPA is responsible for issuing guidelines and regulations for the proper management of solid and hazardous waste (includ-
ing mixed [radioactive and hazardous] waste). In NYS, the EPA has delegated the authority to issue permits and enforce these
regulations to NYSDEC. In addition, the NY Department of Transportation (DOT) is responsible for issuing guidelines and regula-
tions for labeling, packaging, and reporting spills of hazardous and mixed wastes while in transit.

The WVDP is a hazardous waste generator requiring RCRA permitting, RCRA corrective actions, and routine RCRA reporting of
hazardous waste activities to NYSDEC. An agreement between the DOE, NYSERDA, EPA, and NYSDEC (the §3008[h]
Consent Order) directs the implementation of the WVDP RCRA corrective action program. (See the inset, "RCRA Permit and
§3008(h) Consent Order History at the WVDP" on the previous page.)

Routine Reporting Required Under RCRA

Quarterly Status Reports, RCRA §3008(h) Consent Order (Docket No. II RCRA §3008(h) 92-0202). Under the Consent Order
signed in 1992, the DOE transmits two quarterly reports to the EPA and NYSDEC:

(1) A progress report summarizing all Consent Order activities at the WVDP for the previous quarter.

(2) A groundwater exception report, summarizing RCRA groundwater monitoring results that exceed established trigger levels
 and an update on the performance of the NDA interceptor trench, geomembrane cover, and slurry wall.

The progress report includes recent activities associated with hazardous waste management, contacts with local community
interest groups and regulatory agencies, and an inventory of mixed waste generated from decontamination, deactivation, and
deconstruction activities during the reporting period. The groundwater exception report also includes an update on the perfor-
mance of the NDA interceptor trench, geomembrane cover, and slurry wall.

2-8 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

Status Reports, RCRA Section 3016. The RCRA 3016 statute requires all federal hazardous facilities to report the current status
of their hazardous waste activities to the EPA and NYS. The annual/biennial hazardous waste reports are submitted through the
EPA’s RCRAInfo Industry Application web-based system.

Hazardous Waste Management. Hazardous wastes at the WVDP are managed in accordance with 6 NYCRR Part 373 and
reported to NYSDEC in the WVDP’s Annual Hazardous Waste Report. This report specifies the quantities of waste generated,
treated, and/or disposed of, and identifies the treatment, storage, and disposal facilities used for the waste. If less than 25 tons
of hazardous waste is generated in a year, no annual Hazardous Waste Reduction Plan is required. (Note: Waste generated from
deconstruction, construction, or spill cleanup is exempt from hazardous waste reduction plans.)

Hazardous and universal waste is shipped off site to RCRA-permitted solid waste management facilities. Some of the universal
waste (i.e., lead-acid batteries and spent lamps) are reclaimed or recycled at off-site, authorized reclamation and recycling
facilities. (See Table 2-3.)

Mixed Waste Management. Mixed waste (a waste that is both radioactive and RCRA hazardous) is also shipped off site. Mixed
wastes that cannot be treated or disposed of within one year are managed according to the Site Treatment Plan (STP), prepared
by the WVDP under requirements of the Federal Facilities Compliance Act (FFCA) (an amendment to RCRA), in accordance with
the Consent Order. The annually updated plan describes the development of treatment capabilities and technologies for treat-
ing mixed waste and updates the mixed waste inventory. Currently, the only wastes covered under the WVDP STP are mixed
transuranic (TRU) waste stored in containers and mixed high activity and TRU residual waste remaining in tank 8D-4, for which
off-site disposal options are still unavailable and for which on-site treatment is impractical.

Installation of video inspection equipment to verify the liquid level of tank 8D-4 and an interior video inspection were com-
pleted in February 2024. Most of the year was spent developing, testing, and refining a sludge sampling system that could
obtain samples down to the bottom of the tank.

Depths of the sludge in different sectors of tank 8D-4 were measured in September 2024 with a range of 14.9 to 18.3 inches
(37.8 to 46.5 cm) deep, which includes an approximately 2-inch thick floc layer above firmer, consolidated sludge. The overall
average sludge depth was 16.4 inches (41.6 cm). Ten sludge samples from different locations in the tank were obtained between
October and December 2024, and five will be shipped to an off-site laboratory in January 2025 for radioisotope and general
chemistry analyses. Characterization of 8D-4 tank contents will continue in 2025, and the collected data will support the evalu-
ation of treatment options for the high-activity sludge and liquid waste in the tank.

RCRA Waste Management Activities for 2024

Per the Consent Order requirements, quarterly progress reports were submitted to the EPA and NYSDEC, documenting progress
on decontamination activities for SWMUs and waste generation activities. A Hazardous Waste Reduction Plan Annual Status
Report was not required by NYSDEC in 2024 because the amount of reportable, non-deconstruction, hazardous waste gener-
ated was less than the 25-ton threshold. Groundwater monitoring, as recommended in the RFI reports and approved by the EPA
and NYSDEC, continued during 2024 per the Consent Order requirements. This included submitting the quarterly RCRA ground-
water exception reports to the EPA and NYSDEC. The groundwater program and monitoring results at the WVDP are discussed
in Chapter 6, "Groundwater Protection Program."

The WVDP continued to have open communications with NYSDEC and the EPA through monthly RCRA teleconferences. The
WVDP is continuing to operate according to the 6 NYCRR Part 373-3, Part A (Interim Status) Permit Application and the RCRA
§3008(h) Administrative Order on Consent.

Other highlights of the RCRA work performed in 2024 are:

NDA and the NDA Interim Measures (IMs). The NDA IM continued to perform as designed, helping to contain the buried waste,
while minimizing the potential for the release of chemical and radiological contaminants to the environment. The volume of
water pumped from the NDA interceptor trench has decreased noticeably since the removal of the Liquid Pretreatment System

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 2-9

(LPS) building and the covering the footprint of the building with a new geomembrane in May 2019. The volume pumped from
the NDA trench in 2024 was 10,732 gallons (gal) (40,625 liters [L]), a 32% decrease compared to the volume pumped in 2023
(15,789 gal [59,768 L]). The volume pumped in 2024 was about 98.4% below the pre-IM volumes of over 700,000 gal/year.

RCRA Inspection. In February 2024, the representatives of the NYSDEC visited the WVDP and conducted the RCRA inspection
of the DOE interim status units (LSA #3 and #4). The representatives walked through the facilities guided by the WVDP and DOE-
WVDP personnel. The inspection identified no observations.

RCRA Closure Certification. The demolition of the A&PC Hot Cells was completed, and the final RCRA Hazardous Waste Closure
Certification Report for A&PC was submitted to NYDEC and accepted. The Hazardous Waste Closure Certification report was
submitted for the Lag Storage Area (LSA) #2 Hardstand.

HLW Tank. The T&VDS, installed in the WTF to maintain the tanks and control corrosion, continued to operate during 2024. The
T&VDS reduced the residual liquid in tank 8D-4 in 2024 by 285 gal (1,079 L). At the end of 2024, 3,047 gal (11,533 L) of liquid
and solids remained in tank 8D-4. The inoperable steam jet was removed from tank 8D-4 in January 2024 and disposed of as
low-level radioactive waste. Three of the six tank liquid samples obtained in late 2023 from different tank depths were shipped
off site for radioisotope and general chemistry analyses in February 2024, with the results received in March. The liquid samples
showed an acceptable pH of 10.0 for corrosion control.

Compliance for Decommissioning

The National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA)

NEPA requires federal agencies, including the DOE, to consider the environmental effects of their proposed actions. Evaluations
are performed to assess potential environmental effects associated with proposed project activities. The level of evaluation and
documentation depends upon whether the action constitutes a major federal action significantly affecting the quality of the
human environment within the meaning of NEPA. Since the WVDP began, a number of proposed site activities have warranted
environmental impact evaluations. A comprehensive summary of the NEPA documents published over the years is included in
Table 2-8. WVDP Environmental Assessment (EA) and EISs can be found at the URL below under the documents index.
https://www.energy.gov/wvdp/west-valley-demonstration-project-homepage

The 2010 FEIS provides the blueprint for all activities currently underway at the WVDP. No new major NEPA documents were
initiated in 2024. The inset text on page 2-12 provides an overview of NEPA and the EIS relevant to the WVDP. For the WVDP
decommissioning timeline, please see Figure 1-3 in Chapter 1, "Introduction."

NRC Work Instruction Packages (WIP) and MPPB Deconstruction
The 1981 Memorandum of Understanding (MOU), mandated by the WVDP Act (See Appendix H), established the NRC's role
in reviewing and providing consultation for the activities conducted at the WNYNSC and the DOE. The agreement applies to all
aspects of construction, operations, and demolition and decontamination (D&D) activities at the WVDP. Upon completion of
the Phase 1 Decommissioning Plan (DP) in 2010, the NRC required the preparation of work plans for the decommissioning and
deconstruction of the Vitrification Facility (VF) and the MPPB. These plans are used to define the requirements and sequencing
of the deconstruction work. Work Instruction Packages (WIPs) provide the full work details needed to complete the decon-
struction of these facilities. The deconstruction of the MPPB was performed in compliance with the MPPB D&D plan, which
was reviewed by the NRC and follows the WIPs. Following the start of deconstruction in September 2022, the MPPB decon-
struction WIP was periodically updated to modify the required work and to add additional clarification and details involved in
the planned deconstruction process.

The NRC Region 1 conducted on-site visits in March and November 2024. The representatives conducted field observations,
reviewed work planning documents, conducted plant walkdowns, and held interviews with site personnel. The NRC concluded
that the Phase 1 Decommissioning was in progress in accordance with the approved planning. For the history of document
transmittal to the NRC at the WVDP, refer to the inset on page 2-13, "Phase 1 Decommissioning NRC Requirements."

2-10 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

Phase 2 Decisionmaking and SEIS Preparation

The DOE and NYSERDA established the Cooperative Agreement to implement the WVDP Act in 1980. In 1990, the first supple-
mental agreement was signed by DOE and NYSERDA to prepare a joint EIS. As stated in Chapter 1, "Introduction," NYSERDA
and DOE continued to work on the PPA modeling, which is part of the EIS preparation, in 2024. The modeling results will be
evaluated in the SEIS and are expected to support the Phase 2 decisions.

 Lake 2, one of the two lakes (reservoirs), located about 0.5 miles south of the MPPB
 (photo credit: A. Carr)

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 2-11

 National Environmental Policy Act of 1969 (NEPA) and EIS
  The categories of the NEPA documentation include categorical exclusion (CX), environmental assessment (EA), and EIS.
  CXs describe actions that will not have a significant effect on the environment. EAs are used to evaluate the extent to
  which a proposed action, not categorically excluded, will affect the environment. Based on the analyses presented in an
  EA and considering regulatory agency, stakeholder, and public comments, the DOE may determine that a proposed action
  is not a major federal action significantly affecting the quality of the human environment within the meaning of NEPA.
  Consequently, the DOE may issue a notice indicating the finding of no significant impact (FONSI) and therefore would not
  require the preparation of an EIS.

  If a proposed action has the potential for significant en-
  vironmental effects, an EIS would be prepared that de-
  scribes proposed alternatives to the action and explains
  the effects of each. Based on the analyses presented
  and considering regulatory agency and public input, the
  DOE will determine the preferred alternative and issue
  a ROD regarding the action.

  Final Decommissioning Environmental Impact State-
  ment (FEIS). In January 2010, the DOE and NYSERDA
  issued the “Final Environmental Impact Statement for
  Decommissioning and/or Long-Term Stewardship at the
  West Valley Demonstration Project and Western New
  York Nuclear Service Center,” DOE/EIS-0226. In the FEIS,
  the DOE and NYSERDA evaluated four alternatives: Site-
  wide Removal, Site-wide Close-In-Place, Phased De-
  cisionmaking, and No Action. Phased Decisionmaking
  was identified as the preferred alternative. Under this
  alternative, decommissioning will be conducted in two
  phases as shown in Figure 1-3. Decommissioning the
  MPPB is part of the Phase 1 EIS work.

  Record of Decision (ROD). On April 14, 2010, the DOE issued the ROD for the FEIS, selecting the phased decisionmaking
  alternative. During Phase 1 Site Decommissioning, a number of highly contaminated facilities are being removed under a
  facilities disposition contract awarded in 2011. The original estimated cost for all of the Phase 1 work was approximately
  $1.2 billion (FEIS, 2010). Phase 1 was estimated to take up to 10 years, during which time DOE will manage the site’s
  remaining facilities in a safe manner. Phase 1B contract awarded in 2025 has a 10-year ordering period with a maximum
  value of up to $3.0 billion that can be performed for up to 15 years.

  Phase 1 Studies. Phase 1 studies were scientific studies conducted to facilitate interagency consensus necessary for the
  completion of decommissioning the remaining facilities following the completion of Phase 1. The Phase 1 studies provide
  technical evaluations supporting the SEIS preparation. The final reports on these studies, published in 2018, are available
  at https://wvphaseonestudies.emcbc.doe.gov/php/pas-update.html.

  Supplemental EIS (SEIS). After completing an EIS (the 2010 FEIS) and issuing an ROD in 2010 for Phase 1 decisions, the
  DOE and NYSERDA determined that the preparation of an SEIS would further the purposes of NEPA and SEQRA (State
  Environmental Quality Review Act). The Phase 2 decision, which will be informed by the SEIS, will determine the decom-
  missioning approach for the remaining facilities. The SEIS will evaluate a range of alternatives for the remaining facilities
  for which it has decommissioning responsibility including removal, in-place closure, and a combination of those two.
  SC&A, Inc. is preparing the SEIS under a DOE contract awarded in April 2017 that was modified in 2022. A draft SEIS is
  expected to be released in 2027 with a public hearing and comments period to follow.

2-12 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 Phase 1 Decommissioning NRC Requirements
  Decommissioning Plan documents for the NRC are required under the Phase 1 decommissioning and facility disposition
  scope of work. These planning documents must be consistent with the preferred alternative in the EIS ROD and NYSERDA
  Findings statement.

  Phase 1 Decommissioning Plan (DP) for the WVDP

  On December 5, 2008, the DOE issued the “Phase 1 Decommissioning Plan for the West Valley Demonstration Project,
  West Valley, NY” (73 Federal Register [FR] 74162) and transmitted it for NRC review. The DP was prepared to meet WVDP
  obligations to the NRC as directed under the WVDP Act.

  The DP addressed Phase 1 of the proposed two-phased approach for WVDP decommissioning, consistent with the pre-
  ferred alternative selected in the ROD (see the inset, "National Environmental Policy Act of 1969 (NEPA) and EIS," for a sum-
  mary of the general NEPA process) and the Findings Statement for the WVDP and the WNYNSC. On December 18, 2009, the
  DOE submitted revision 2 of the Phase 1 DP after incorporating responses to NRC’s comments.

  On February 25, 2010, the NRC transmitted to DOE-WVDP the Technical Evaluation Report (TER) for the Phase 1 DP, con-
  cluding that the Phase 1 DP was consistent with the preferred alternative in the EIS prepared for NEPA. The NRC also
  determined that there is reasonable assurance that the proposed Phase 1 actions will meet the decommissioning criteria.

  Phase 1 Characterization Sampling and Analysis Plan (CSAP) and the Phase 1 Final Status Survey Plan (FSSP) for the
  WVDP

  The Phase 1 DP required the preparation of two supplemental documents, the CSAP and the FSSP. These two documents
  provide the specific details of sampling activities to support Phase 1 decommissioning of the WVDP. The CSAP describes the
  radiological environmental data collection activities (surface and subsurface soils, sediments, and groundwater) that will
  specifically support the implementation of the Phase 1 decommissioning actions within the WVDP premises as described
  in the Phase 1 DP.

  The FSSP provides the technical basis and sampling protocols to demonstrate that specific portions of the WVDP premises
  meet the Phase 1 radiological cleanup goals for surface and subsurface soils identified in the Phase 1 DP. The FSSP is con-
  sistent with the Multi-Agency Radiation Survey and Site Investigation Manual (MARSSIM).

Other Compliance Related Updates
Occurrence Report. Under the DOE O 232.2A Chg 1, DOE nuclear sites are required to notify DOE personnel, including National
Nuclear Security Administration (NNSA) personnel, about events that could adversely affect the health and safety of the public
or the workers, the environment, DOE missions, or the credibility of the Department. The WVDP submitted two notifications
to the Occurrence Reporting and Processing System (ORPS) in 2024. Those events pertained to the Lockout/Tagout (LOTO)
procedure and the preventive maintenance system for the Petroleum Bulk Storage (PBS) program, respectively. Subsequent
fact-finding analyses identified procedural weaknesses, and corrective measures were implemented.

Emergency Planning and Community Right-to-Know Act (EPCRA). The EPCRA, also known as the Superfund Amendments and
Reauthorization Act (SARA) Title III, is a federal law passed in 1986 to inform the public of potential environmental and hazards
posed by the storage and handling of hazardous or toxic chemicals at facilities in their communities.

The WVDP is required to maintain Safety Data Sheets (SDSs) that describe the properties and health effects of all chemicals
used and stored on site. Annual reporting requirements are based on the types and quantities of these potentially hazardous
or toxic chemicals.

In 2024, the WVDP did not use or store any EPCRA-listed extremely hazardous substances exceeding their threshold planning
quantity. The WVDP did continue to use and store hazardous chemicals on site. As shown in Table 2-4, the only report required
under SARA Title III in 2024 was the hazardous chemical inventory. The 2024 inventory of chemicals stored at the WVDP is pro-
vided in Table 2-5. This chemical inventory was provided to state and local emergency response organizations and the nearby
local fire departments.

Migratory Bird Treaty Act. The Migratory Bird Treaty Act provides for the protection of migratory birds, their nests, and their
eggs. The DOE maintains a Bird Depredation permit that allows for the removal of bird nests from areas where they would
be impacted by site operations. Under the Bird Depredation permit, an additional registration is required for the removal or
destruction of Canada geese nests and/or goose eggs. Table 2-6 summarizes the migratory bird activities conducted during
2024.

The Endangered Species Act and the National Historic Preservation Act. Several ecological surveys of the WNYNSC premises
have been conducted over the years. Except for occasional transient individuals, no plant or animal species protected under
the Endangered Species Act of 1973 resides at the WNYNSC premises. Surveys of the historic and archaeological sites revealed
Native American and historic homestead artifacts on the WNYNSC premises and in the nearby areas. None of the sites were
impacted by the WVDP activities in 2024.

Invasive Species Control. Federal agencies are required to prevent the introduction, establishment and spread of invasive
species, as well as to eradicate and control populations of invasive species according to EO 13751 Safeguarding the Nation from
the Impacts of Invasive Species. In 2023, wild parsnip, which can cause burns, was identified along the fence by the south park-
ing lot and removed. Additionally, less harmful invasive species seen on site, including multiflora rose, phragmites, nonnative
honeysuckle, European starlings, Japanese knotweed, and house sparrows. These species were continue to be monitored for
potential concerns in 2024.

Floodplain Management. EO 11988, Floodplain Management, was issued to avoid adverse impacts associated with the occu-
pancy and modification of floodplains. The WVDP is required to report when it develops new floodplains or conducts activities
that would impact the existing 100-year floodplain within the premises. No such development or activities were performed in
2024.

Project Assessment Compliance
Project assessments are conducted through the Integrated Assessment Program (IAP) at the WVDP. This program effectively
complies with applicable DOE directives, regulations, and standards, and Integrated Safety Management System (ISMS) as well
as Environmental Management System (EMS) requirements. Chapter 3, "Environmental Management System" describes these
assessment projects at the WVDP in detail.

The Construction and Demolition Debris Landfill (CDDL) and Permeable Treatment Wall (PTW). The overall condition of the
CDDL grounds was inspected in March and September 2024 with minor ground maintenance performed. The CDDL has been
closed since 1986 under a NYSDEC-approved closure plan for a nonradioactive solid waste disposal facility.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 2-15

Over time, the north plateau groundwater plume has migrated from the MPPB into the CDDL area and beyond. In 2010, a
full-scale PTW was installed south of the CDDL. Construction of the PTW did not impact the CDDL. Routine inspections of the
PTW area were also performed in 2024. Additional discussion of the PTW is provided in Chapter 6, "Groundwater Monitoring
Program" of this report.

The WNYNSC Lakes/Canal/Dams/Spillway System. The two lakes (#1 and #2), or reservoirs, canal connecting the two lakes,
a spillway for discharging overflow from Lake #1 to Buttermilk Creek, and two dams are located on the WNYNSC property. The
lakes/dams/canal/spillway system was conceived and designed in the late 1950s through the early 1960s. The system has been
maintained to provide water for fire protection and SPDES discharge flow augmentation for the WVDP. The WVDP rail spur and
an access roadway are located parallel to the lakes and run along the crest of both dams.

The lakes, dams, canal, and culverts are inspected by Plant Systems Operations (PSO) operators weekly and by site engineer-
ing personnel twice every year. The Lake #1 spillway (shown in Figure A-4)was inspected by Regulatory Strategy personnel of
CHBWV monthly in 2024. In recent years, the dams and a spillway have suffered progressive erosion. An independent engineer-
ing review of the lakes and dams was performed in 2021 by McMahon and Mann Consulting Engineers, Inc. In 2023, a dam
meeting with NYSDEC personnel was held, and the NYSDEC performed an inspection of the dams, canal, and spillway. The DOE,
together with the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers (USACE), evaluated the NYSDEC's inspection results and made engineering rec-
ommendations for repairs and improvements. A final design of the spillway reinforcement was proposed by the USACE in 2024.
The contract finalization and Lake 1 spillway improvement construction will occur in 2025.

The railway continued to be used in 2024 for the transport of wastes off site. Routine maintenance and inspections of the rail-
way also continued to be performed in 2025.

2-24 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024
