 Citation

 42 United States Code The Atomic Energy Act (AEA) of 1954 was enacted to assure the proper management of
 (USC) §2011 et seq. source, special nuclear, and by‐product materials. The AEA and the statutes that amended it
 delegate the control of nuclear energy primarily to DOE, NRC, and EPA. See discussions of the
 WVDP Act, DOE Orders 435.1, and 458.1 below.
 Public Law The WVDP Act of 1980 authorized DOE to carry out a HLW demonstration project at the
 96‐368 WNYNSC (the Center) in West Valley, New York.
 Cooperative Agreement The Cooperative Agreement between DOE and NYSERDA established a cooperative framework
 between DOE and for implementing the WVDP Act, effective October 1980, as amended in September 1981. In
 NYSERDA 1990, the first supplemental agreement was signed by DOE and NYSERDA which set forth
 specific provisions for preparing a joint EIS. The DOE ROD for the FEIS was issued in April 2010
 for the WVDP. A second supplemental agreement to the Cooperative Agreement was drafted in
 January 2010 and issued by DOE and NYSERDA in March 2011.
 WVDP MOU between The 1981 Memorandum of Understanding (MOU), mandated by the WVDP Act, established
 DOE and NRC procedures for review and consultation by NRC with respect to activities conducted at the
 WNYNSC by DOE. The agreement encompassed development, design, construction, operation,
 and D&D activities associated with the Project as described in the WVDP Act. Under the WVDP
 Act, and to satisfy commitments made to NRC, DOE was required to prepare a DP for the
 Project and submit it to NRC for review.
 DOE Order 231.1B DOE Order 231.1B Chg 1, Environment, Safety, and Health Reporting (updated and approved
 on June 27, 2011 with Change 1 issued on November 28, 2012), was issued to ensure that DOE
 and National Nuclear Security Administration receives timely and accurate information about
 events that could adversely affect the health, safety, and security of the public or workers, the
 environment, the operations of DOE facilities, or the credibility of the Department.

 This is accomplished through timely collection, reporting, analysis, and dissemination of data
 pertaining to environment, safety, and health issues as required by law or regulations, or in
 support of U.S. political commitments to the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA).
 DOE Order 232.2A DOE Order 232.2A, Occurrence Reporting and Processing (ORP) of Operations Information
 defines requirements to notify DOE about events that could adversely affect the public, the
 workers, or the environment.
 DOE Order 458.1 DOE Order 458.1 Chg 4, Radiation Protection of the Public and the Environment established
 requirements to protect the public and environment against undue risk from radiation
 associated with radiological activities conducted under control of DOE pursuant to the AEA, by
 ensuring that:
 (1) operations are conducted to limit radiation exposure to members of the public pursuant to
 limits established in the Order,
 (2) radiological clearance of DOE real and personal property is controlled,
 (3) potential radiation exposures to members of the public are as low as reasonably achievable
 (ALARA),
 (4) routine and nonroutine releases are monitored and dose to the public is assessed, and
 (5) the environment is protected from the effects of radiation and radioactive material.

 DOE Order 435.1 DOE M 435.1 Chg 2, Radioactive Waste Management ensures that all DOE radioactive waste is
 managed in a manner that is protective of worker and public health and safety and the
 environment, and complies with applicable state, federal, and local laws and regulations. Under
 the Order, sites that manage radioactive waste are required to develop, document, implement,
 and maintain a site‐wide radioactive waste management program which includes actions to
 minimize radioactive waste generation.
 Title 10 CFR Part 830, 10 CFR Part 830, Nuclear Safety Management, Subpart A, Quality Assurance Requirements,
 Subpart A, DOE Order and DOE Order 414.1D Chg 2, Quality Assurance, provide the quality assurance (QA) program
 414.1D Chg2 policies and requirements applicable to WVDP activities.
 42 USC §4321 et seq., The National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) of 1969 and as amended in 1970, established a
 and national policy to ensure that protection of the environment is included in federal planning and
 10 CFR Part 1021 decisionmaking. The President's Council on Environmental Quality established a screening
 system of analyses and documentation that requires each proposed action to be categorized
 according to the extent of its potential environmental impact.
 Environmental The NY State Environmental Quality Review (SEQR) Act of January 1, 1996, enacted in
 Conservation Law (ECL), September 1976 and as amended on June 26, 2000, requires adequate environmental review
 6 NYCRR Part 617 NYS and assessment of whether a proposed action has the potential to have a significant
 environmental impact, prior to a decision regarding the action. Where a project involves both
 NYS and federal approvals, it is preferred to coordinate the SEQR and NEPA processes.

 Coordinated efforts were made at the WVDP to effectively utilize information from the federal
 EIS process to make the required SEQR Findings Statement for the WVDP and WNYNSC, which
 was issued in May 2010.
 42 USC §6901 et seq., The RCRA of 1976 and the NYS Solid Waste Disposal Act (NYS ECL Article 27 [Title 9]) govern
 and NYS ECL, 6 NYCRR the generation, storage, handling, and disposal of hazardous wastes and closure of systems that
 Chapter 4, Subchapter B handle these wastes. RCRA was enacted to ensure that hazardous wastes are managed in a way
 Part 373 that protects human health, safety, and the environment.
 Amendment to 42 USC The Federal Facility Compliance Act (FFCA) of 1992 (an amendment to RCRA) requires DOE
 §6961, NYS ECL, and facilities to prepare an Site Treatment Plan (STP) for treating mixed waste inventories to meet
 NYSDEC Administrative land disposal restrictions (LDR) and to annually update the plan to account for changes in mixed
 Order on Consent with waste inventories, capacities, and treatment technologies. DOE entered into a Consent Order
 DOE with NYSDEC for the WVDP in 1996.
 Docket No. II RCRA DOE and NYSERDA entered into the RCRA §3008(h) Administrative Order on Consent with EPA
 §3008(h) 92‐0202, and (lead agency) and NYSDEC in March 1992. The state and federal RCRA regulations authorize the
 NYS ECL agencies to issue orders requiring RCRA corrective actions associated with the potential releases
 of hazardous waste and/or hazardous constituents from WVDP Solid Waste Management Units
 (SWMUs) (under DOE jurisdiction) and WNYNSC SWMUs (under NYSERDA jurisdiction).

 RCRA Section 3016 The RCRA 3016 Statute applies to all federal hazardous waste facilities currently owned or
 operated by the government. It requires that the status of facility hazardous waste activities be
 reported to EPA and are authorized every two years. WVDP facility hazardous waste activities
 are reported biennially to EPA and NYSDEC.
 42 USC §7401 et seq.; The Clean Air Act (CAA) of 1970 and the NYS ECL regulate the release of air pollutants through
 40 CFR 61, Subpart H; permits, approvals, and air quality limits. Emissions of radionuclides are regulated by EPA via
 and the NESHAP regulations. On April 5, 1995, the DOE and the EPA entered into an MOU
 6 NYCRR Chapter 3, Air concerning the CAA Emission Standards for Radionuclides, 40 CFR Part 61, including Subparts H,
 Resources I, Q, and T. Nonradiological emissions are regulated under 6 NYCRR Chapter 3 Part 201‐4
 (Minor Facility Registrations).

 At the WVDP, the DOE has the EPA approval to release radiological emissions from four active
 stacks and up to 15 Portable Ventilation Units (PVUs). The DOE also maintains a NYS Air Facility
 Registration Certificate for nonradiological sources.
 33 USC §1251 et seq. The Federal Water Pollution Control Act of 1977 (Clean Water Act [CWA]) and NYS ECL (Article
 and 17 [Title 8]) seek to improve surface water quality by establishing standards and a system of
 NYS ECL and permits. Wastewater and stormwater discharges are regulated by NYSDEC through the SPDES
 6 NYCRR Chapter 10, permit. Discharges of fill material are regulated through permits issued by the U.S. Army Corps
 Division of Water of Engineers (USACE) and water quality certifications issued by NYSDEC.

 NYSDEC has granted the WVDP permission to continue operating under the current SPDES
 permit, with an effective date of July 1, 2011, based on the project's timely and compliant
 renewal application per the provisions of the State Administrative Procedure Act (SAPA).
 NYS ECL Article 17 (Titles NYS ECL Article 17 (Titles 7 and 8), and ECL Article 70 regulate stormwater discharges related to
 7 and 8) and Article 70 construction activity.
 NYS Navigation Law and NYS ECL Article 17 (Titles 10 and 17), 6 NYCRR 612–614 and Parts 595–599, and 6 NYCRR
 NYS ECL Article 17 (Titles Subpart 360‐14 regulate design, operation, inspection, maintenance, and closure of
 10 and 17) aboveground and underground petroleum bulk storage (PBS) and chemical bulk storage (CBS)
 tanks. These laws also regulate spill reporting and cleanup. Under terms of a 1996 agreement,
 amended in 2005, DOE is not required to report a spill of petroleum product onto an impervious
 surface if the spill is less than five gallons and is cleaned up within two hours of discovery.
 Minor petroleum spills that do not meet these conditions are reported quarterly to NYSDEC.
 Spills of larger significance may have immediate reporting requirements.

 The WVDP has five registered PBS tanks (four aboveground storage tanks [ASTs] and one
 underground storage tank [UST]). They are inspected monthly and maintained. Spills are
 reported and cleaned up in accordance with WVDP policies and procedures.

 EO 11990 and NYS ECL EO 11990, Protection of Wetlands, directed federal agencies to avoid, where possible, impacts
 (e.g., destruction, modification, or new construction) that would adversely affect wetlands
 wherever there is a practical alternative. Activities in wetlands are regulated by the USACE and
 NYSDEC permits. The wetlands on the WVDP are subject to regulation under Section 404 of the
 CWA and NYS ECL Articles 24 and 36.

 The most recent site‐wide WVDP wetlands survey was performed in 2003 and approved by
 USACE in March 2006. Additional wetlands were delineated in the vicinity of the firing range in
 October 2006 and in the vicinity of the HLW Cask Storage Pad and NDA in May 2013.
 42 USC §9601 et seq. The Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act of 1980
 (CERCLA), including the Superfund Amendments and Reauthorization Act of 1986 [SARA])
 provided the regulatory framework for remediation of releases of hazardous substances and
 remediation of inactive hazardous waste disposal sites. If a hazardous substance spill exceeds a
 reportable quantity, CERCLA reporting requirements are triggered.

 Based on the results of a Preliminary Assessment Report prepared for DOE, it was determined
 that the WVDP did not qualify for listing on the National Priorities List. Therefore, no further
 investigation pursuant to CERCLA was warranted.
 42 USC §11001 et seq. The Emergency Planning and Community Right‐to‐Know Act (EPCRA) of 1986 (also known as
 SARA Title III) was designed to create a working partnership between industry, business, state,
 and local government, and emergency response representatives to help local communities
 protect public health, safety, and the environment from chemical hazards.
 42 USC §300f et seq. The Safe Drinking Water Act of 1974 requires that each federal agency operating or
 maintaining a public water system must comply with all federal, state, and local requirements
 regarding safe drinking water. Compliance in NYS is verified by oversight of the NYSDOH,
 through NYS Public Health Law, and the Cattaraugus County Health Department (CCHD).
 40 CFR Part 141 EPA published a regulation in 1991 to control lead and copper in drinking water. This regulation
 Subpart I is known as the Lead and Copper Rule (also referred to as the LCR). In October 2024, EPA issued
 the Lead and Copper Rule Improvements (LCRI) final rule, which updates various aspects of the
 LCR, including requiring public water systems to replace all lead and galvanized requiring
 replacement (GRR) service lines within 10 years and setting minimum annual service line
 replacement rates. The LCRI also lowered the lead action level from 15 μg/L to 10 μg/L.

 §141.84(a) (Service line inventory and replacement requirements) requires that all water
 systems must develop a service line inventory that identifies the material, including lead, and
 location of each service line connected to the public water distribution.
 10 CFR Part 851 10 CFR 851 Worker Safety and Health Program of 2006 requires DOE contractors to provide
 workers with a safe and healthful workplace. To accomplish this objective, the rule established
 program requirements specific to management responsibilities, worker rights, hazard
 identification and prevention, safety health standards, required training, recordkeeping, and
 reporting. Worker safety and training are discussed in Chapter 3, "Environmental Management
 System."

 6 NYCRR 601.5 6 NYCRR 601.5 Water Withdrawal Reporting requires that any person who withdraws or is

2-30 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 CHAPTER 3
  ENVIRONMENTAL MANAGEMENT SYSTEM

EMS recertification: The CHBWV EMS was recommended for certification under the ISO 14001:2015 standard following the
successful completion of the annual third-party EMS recertification audit in April 2024. The WVDP scored 100% (which equates
to the highest score of “green”) on the federal EMS performance metrics scorecard for 2024, indicating that the site has a robust
EMS.

Energy Usage: The natural gas usage for FY 2024 was approximately 4.1% lower than that of FY 2023. Total potable water usage
decreased by 6.3% in FY 2024 as compared to FY 2023. Industrial, Landscaping, and Agricultural (ILA) water use increased 19.4%
from FY 2023 to FY 2024.

Procurement Practices: WVDP acquisition and procurement practices continue to ensure that 100% of all eligible electronics
were Electronic Product Environmental Assessment Tool (EPEAT) registered. In FY 2024, the WVDP purchased approximately
$131,651 of products containing recycled or biobased materials.

Environmental Management System (EMS)
The DOE is committed to implementing sound stewardship practices to protect the air, water, land, and other natural and cul-
tural resources that may be affected by its activities at the WVDP. Environmental requirements and pollution prevention were
incorporated into the WVDP’s work planning and execution, and actions were taken to minimize the environmental impact of
the operations, in accordance with its environmental policy.

An EMS is a management practice that enables an organization to conduct its work in a systematic manner, minimizing the envi-
ronmental impacts of its operations. The WVDP EMS was designed to meet ISO 14001:2015 (the Environmental Management
Systems [EMS] Standard). An effective EMS ensures that appropriate operational procedures and environmental monitoring
programs are in place to minimize or eliminate any potential environmental impact from each project. Establishing the EMS
also helps systematically address regulatory requirements, reducing the risk of noncompliance and improving environmental
performance by ensuring that environmental evaluations include effective communication, adhere to all applicable regulatory
guidance, and include necessary regulatory notifications and approvals.

At the WVDP, the EMS is incorporated into all planned work activities. The scope of EMS in 2024 focused on the continued
deconstruction and demolition of the remaining above-ground structure of the MPPB, which was conducted in a manner safe
for the workers and the surrounding environment. The 2024 scope also included the management and shipment of radiologi-
cally contaminated waste generated prior to the commencement of deconstruction.

An independent third-party EMS ISO 14001:2015 certification audit conducted by Orion Group in April 2024 revealed no non-
conformities, areas of concern, or opportunities for improvement. Two strengths (engaged leadership team and excellent com-
munication program) were noted during the audit for the certification. An internal audit of the EMS for the ISO 14001:2015
standard was conducted in August 2024 by the WVDP Performance Assurance department. During the audit, CHBWV was
commended for the WVDP teams’ promptness and thoroughness in addressing project needs and environmental concerns,
including reducing emissions, lowering the site’s carbon footprint, and minimizing water and fuel usage, as well as their impact
on the surrounding wetlands.

Communications. CHBWV actively participates in the DOE-HQ Office of Sustainable Environmental Stewardship, an organization
that fosters the sharing of EMS best practices. Additionally, the WVDP maintains its routine and frequent communications with
regulatory agencies, including teleconferences with NYSDEC and the EPA on RCRA projects and RCRA path forward meetings.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 3-1

videos in 2021 and 2022:

 • The Environmental Monitoring team providing details on the site’s state-of-the-art monitoring system and verifies the
 protective measures and safeguards in place that continue to protect employees, the public, and the environment.

 • Subject Matter Experts (SMEs) describing the safe and compliant approach being employed to deconstruct this facility.

 • An animation showing the equipment used to deconstruct the MPPB, including the dust suppression misters, as well
 as the order in which the rooms will be removed.

implemented to provide monitoring information ahead of the ASER, which, by DOE order, is published annually in October every
year. Links to these electronic communications are below.

Self-Assessments. At the WVDP, self-assessment activities are stressed as a mechanism for evaluating, improving, and main-
taining safety and protection of the public and the environment. These self-assessments are coordinated through the site
Integrated Assessment Program (IAP) and involve an annual review of each functional area of the WVDP.

Deconstruction Activity Monitoring. Since the commencement of the MPPB deconstruction in 2022, the WVDP EMS team has
monitored any changes in the on-site and off-site environment associated with the deconstruction. Monitored activities include
air emissions, waste management, soil, and surface water and groundwater quality.

Lessons Learned. The WVDP participates in the DOE Lessons Learned Program, providing internal training and opportunities
for improvement in safety and environmental stewardship.

Management Review. The routine annual internal review by the EMS Executive Safety Review Board (ESRB) was held in March
2024. The ESRB reviews the site’s environmental performance annually to ensure the continuing suitability, adequacy, and effec-
tiveness of the EMS. The WVDP EMS was determined to be operating effectively during this review.

Policy and Commitment
It is the policy of the WVDP to integrate environmental requirements and pollution prevention into project planning and exe-
cution to ensure that sound environmental stewardship practices are implemented. The WVDP environmental policy for 2024
requires commitments in nine areas.

 • Identify and comply with all applicable environmental laws and regulations;

3-2 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 • Use practicable means to minimize or eliminate the generation of new wastes without a path for disposition;

 • Protect the unique natural, biological and cultural resources associated with WVDP activities;

 • Conserve natural resources by reusing and recycling materials, purchasing recycled materials, and using recyclable
 materials; and

 • Consider the input of our stakeholders when weighing alternative environmental courses of action.

Managers are expected to take prompt action to address environmental concerns and to have zero tolerance for noncompliance
with the policy.

EMS Implementation
The EMS directs that work planning must involve identifying activities with specific regulatory requirements and activities with
the potential for significant environmental impacts. Additionally, planned work must be performed in a manner that contributes
to the DOE’s EMS goals. Incorporating the EMS in the planning stage contributes to successful project outcomes, in terms of the
DOE’s EMS objectives. See Figure 3-1 for the key pillars of the WVDP’s EMS implementation plan.

Regulatory Compliance. Assessment of the applicability of environmental laws and regulations prior to initiating a project
ensures that appropriate permits and operating practices are in place. Compliance is also maintained by routine environmental
monitoring of air, surface water, drinking water, groundwater, and ambient radiation exposure. Required regulatory reports that
analyze these data are submitted to NYSERDA, the EPA, and the DOE.

Environmental Aspect Analysis. For each facility or structure considered for deconstruction, the base environmental aspects
were identified and addressed during work planning, with the assistance of hazard control specialists. An “environmental
aspect” is any element of an organization’s activities, products, or services that can impact the environment.

Activities that have regulatory implications or those that have the potential for significant environmental impacts are identified
as “significant aspects” through a quantitative ranking process, per the ISO 14001:2015 standard.

Identified potential significant aspects were systematically graded for their likelihood of occurring, the potential magnitude
of the impact, the potential regulatory requirements or ramifications, the anticipated level of community concern, and the
resulting potential risk. The purpose of grading environmental aspects is to focus management attention on the most important
environmental concerns associated with the WVDP’s scope of work.

The potential significant environmental aspects identified for 2024 are summarized in Table 3-1. The activities evaluated for
their environmental aspects in 2024 included radiological and asbestos air emissions, radiological waste generation, and toxic
discharge to surface water.

Integrated Safety Management System (ISMS). The EMS is reinforced by the Integrated Safety Management System (ISMS)
(DOE Order 450.2 Chg1). The DOE’s ISMS ensures that the DOE, including the National Nuclear Security Administration (NNSA),
systematically integrates safety into management and work practices at all levels and that missions are accomplished efficiently
while protecting workers, the public, and the environment. EMS and ISMS guidelines help to ensure a safe and environmentally
sound deconstruction plan.

Objectives and Targets. The WVDP establishes environmental aspects and then sets objectives and measurable targets for
improving environmental performance or minimizing potential environmental impacts. Objectives and targets are re-aligned
annually to support upcoming operations and work activities. The WVDP’s EMS objectives in 2024 included minimizing potential
surface water contamination, reducing legacy waste volumes, the removal of the MPPB facility, the purchase of EPEAT products,
and reducing electric usage. EPEAT purchasing and electric usage in 2024 are reported in the “2024 EMS Results” section on the
following page.

Occupational Radiation Protection. The aspects of EMS include not only the impacts on the environment but also the impact
on personnel health and safety. Worker safety and health protection at DOE sites are governed by 10 CFR Part 851, Worker
Safety and Health Program. The program established requirements for management responsibilities, worker rights, hazard
identification, training, record-keeping, and other related aspects. The WVDP implements these rules and requirements in its
operational procedures and continues to review whether its procedures comply with worker protection protocols.

Training. The key elements of an effective EMS include employee training demonstrating leadership’s commitment to proce-
dure compliance and environmental stewardship. The “WVDP Worker Safety and Health Plan” at the WVDP describes required
safety training and explains how the WVDP complies with 10 CFR 851. Based on individual work requirements, employees
receive specialized safety training tailored to their specific needs. For example, employees who work in environments with
airborne hazards must first medically qualify and successfully complete Respiratory Protection training. Those who may work
with asbestos removal take asbestos training. Regulatory compliance personnel involved in waste management are required to
take Hazardous Waste Operations and Emergency Response training.

Safety Record. At the WVDP, all employees participate in human performance/behavior-based safety training to help reduce
errors and prevent accidents. Any person working at the WVDP with a personal photo badge that allows unescorted access to
administrative areas of the site must successfully complete general employee training, which covers health and safety, emer-
gency response, environmental compliance, and other essential topics.

Additionally, the WVDP conducts an annual all-employee safety event. At the event, the employees participate in hands-on
activities and listen to safety lectures that highlight the importance of safety during complex deconstruction activities. The 2024
event was held again in July.

Energy Use Credit. The Renewable Energy Credit (REC) is
a type of EAC that proves electricity was generated from a
renewable source. In FY 2024, the WVDP used 5,629 MWh of electricity to support site activities, and purchased 2,400 MWh,
consisting of certified solar and wind energy, which offset the site’s energy usage by 42.6%. The electric usage in 2024 was 53%
lower, while natural gas was 65% lower than the 2008 baselines. (See Figure 3-2.)

Water Use. All potable and Industrial, Landscaping, and Agricultural (ILA) water is supplied by two groundwater wells except
for the augmentation water required during lagoon discharges, which is supplied by the reservoirs. In FY 2024, approximately
15.6% of the total water used at the WVDP was for potable water and 84.4% was used for industrial purposes. (See Figure 3-3.)
Total water usage increased by 14.0%, potable water use decreased by 6.5%, and ILA water usage increased 18.9% compared to
FY 2023. ILA water increased due to dust suppression during MPPB deconstruction activities.

Pollution Prevention and Waste Reduction. The WVDP “Waste Minimization and Pollution Prevention Awareness Plan”
requires that waste minimization objectives be included in the work instructions for all projects, and encourages procurement
of recycled products, reusing existing products, and using methods that conserve energy. Material recycling and reuse is tracked
under the EMS. Nonradiologically contaminated solid waste generated in FY 2024 was recycled or reused where practical. A
total of approximately 46.82 tons of material was diverted from landfills in FY 2024. The quantity of each type of material recy-
cled, reused, or donated is summarized in Table 3-2 on the next page.

Environmental Resilience. The WVDP has worked to ensure the site has safeguarded against potential challenges by prioritiz-
ing environmental resilience. The WVDP aims meet regulatory requirements as well as provide protection for the surrounding
ecosystems and communities, embodying a commitment to environmental stability and safety. Future environmental distur-
bances, such as soil erosion or flooding caused by extreme weather events, will be modeled in the Probabilistic Performance
Assessment (PPA) and alternatives will be evaluated the Supplemental Environmental Impact Statement (SEIS).

Site Security: As the DOE mission is to complete the WVDP Act and associated waste management activities, a continued em-
phasis will be placed on organizational resilience through site security and emergency preparedness procedures.

Community Outreach
Working With the Seneca Nation. The Seneca Nation is a federally recognized Native nation located approximately 17 miles
downstream and to the west of the WVDP. Cattaraugus Creek runs the extent of the Territory before emptying into Lake Erie.
Due to this, the Cattaraugus Territory community be potentially impacted by activities at the WVDP. This territory of the Seneca
Nation is about 22,000 acres (89 km²).

3-6 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

The DOE and the Seneca Nation signed a Cooperative Agreement in 1996 on the on-site low-level waste disposal as part of
the 1996 initial Environmental Impact Statement (EIS, DOE/EIS-0200-F). The Cooperative Agreement has been instrumental in
fostering the government-to-government relationship between the Seneca Nation and the DOE. The Seneca Nation’s Environ-
mental Protection Department (EPD) manages the DOE Cooperative Agreement on behalf of the Seneca Nation. The Coopera-
tive Agreement has enabled the Seneca Nation to participate in the West Valley Citizen Task Force (CTF) and the DOE State and
Tribal Government Working Group.

The proposed tasks (provided every five years in the form of a work plan and budget request) for the Cooperative Agree-
ment activities continue to promote communication between the Seneca Nation and the DOE regarding environmental, human
health, and cultural resources of the Seneca Nation. The DOE at the WVDP also entered into a Memorandum of Agreement
(MOA) between the Seneca Nation and the DOE. The purpose of the MOA is to provide for notification to the Seneca Nation in
advance of any shipments of the WVDP spent nuclear fuel and high-level waste across the Seneca Nation lands and to provide
for the safe and secure transportation of such material, including the development of culturally sensitive risk management
strategies and emergency planning and response, in coordination with the Seneca Nation.

Community Engagement. The WVDP has worked to establish long-term relationships with local communities and strives to
communicate and engage community stakeholders regularly. The communication plan is focused on keeping internal and exter-
nal stakeholders informed of environmental work and project-related cleanup progress. Some of these communication meth-
ods include community outreach/involvement, project presentations at Quarterly Public Meetings (QPM) and CTF meetings,
and digital communications (links provided in the Communications section of this chapter). Residents from surrounding com-
munities, across all segments of society, can become members of the CTF.

In 2024, WVDP participated in annual events, including the Toys for Tots Program and a food drive for nine local food pantries,
during the holiday season. In total, the WVDP Food Drive has collected more than 2.4 million pounds of food since its inception
in 1989. In April, the WVDP president and general manager and the communications director served as two of the judges at the
Cattaraugus County Environthon, an environmental competition for high school students. At the ENVIROTHON, students tested
their knowledge of soils, aquatics, forestry, wildlife, and current environmental issues in NYS.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 3-7

 A view from Mill Street Bridge over Cattaraugus Creek in winter

3-8 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 CHAPTER 4
 ENVIRONMENTAL MONITORING

Air: The WVDP continued to monitor on-site air and off-site air in 2024. On-site monitoring results at two active stacks and eight
PVUs were well under the DOE Derived Concentration Standards (DCSs) (See the inset, “Radiological Data Evaluation” in this
chapter.) There were very low positive detections of several isotopes in 2024 on the ambient air monitoring network (AAMN)
samplers from the deconstruction activities corresponding with local winds. The estimated maximum dose from those detec-
tions to one of the nearest residents was 1.8% of the NESHAP annual emission limit of 10 mrem/year.

Water: Concentrations of radioisotopes and other water quality indicators in the controlled SPDES discharge were similar to
previous years. Whole Effluent Toxicity (WET) testing continued to be performed during 2024 with results compliant with the
action levels. Above background levels of gross alpha, gross beta, cesium-137, strontium-90, tritium, and uranium isotopes
were detected in surface water on the WVDP premises. However, these concentrations were well below DOE standards. An
annual strontium-90 data indicated a positive detection in creek water samples collected downstream of the WVDP. Again,
these concentrations were multiple orders of magnitude lower than the DCS.

Drinking Water: On-site drinking water continued to remain below the local, state, and federal maximum contaminant levels
(MCLs) and drinking water standards for chemical and biological contaminants. As required by the EPA’s new PFAS monitoring
directive, the WVDP reports six PFAS compound measurements and a hazard index ratio of PFAS mixture in this report, starting
this year. Sampling results were all far below the MCLs and the hazard index result was 0.2 of MCL ratio of 1.

Food Sources: Milk was sampled at a near-site farm and venison samples were collected at near-site locations and background
locations in 2024. In addition to naturally occurring potassium-40, cesium-137 was detected in one background sample and
strontium-90 was detected in one near-site sample. Those detections were within the historical measurement ranges.

Direct Radiation Monitoring: Direct radiation measurements on the WNYNSC perimeter were not significantly different from
background measurements taken in Great Valley, 18 miles south of the site. Direct radiation measurement results on site were
similar to those in previous years.

Environmental Monitoring Program (EMP)
At the WVDP, on-site and off-site air and surface water, drinking water, sediment, soil, venison (white-tailed deer), fish, milk,
and food crop samples are collected under the WVDP EMP. Those sampling locations were carefully selected based on histor-
ical data reviews and consultations with regulatory agencies. These samples are analyzed for radiological and chemical con-
stituents and water quality parameters. Selected off-site locations (called background or control locations) are distant enough
not to be affected by the WVDP activities and are typically located upwind of the site or upstream for surface water samples.

The groundwater monitoring program and potential exposure pathways from the groundwater are discussed separately in
Chapter 6. Figure 4-1 displays the overview of the WVDP environmental monitoring program. A description of the sampling
schedule at each location, monitoring program drivers and rationale, as well as maps showing the sampling locations, are pre-
sented in Appendix A.

On-Site Airborne Emissions Monitoring Program
Point source emissions. The WVDP currently maintains required EPA approvals for radiological releases from three active air
emission points from building ventilation systems (otherwise referred to as “stacks”). This includes the Supernatant Treatment
System (STS) stack (ANSTSTK), the Remote Handled Waste Facility (RHWF) stack (ANRHWFK), and the Replacement Ventilation
System (RVS) stack (ANRVEU1). The ANRVEU1 stack was shut down in 2022. The Container Sorting and Packaging Facility (CSPF)
stack (ANCSPFK) that does not require EPA approval is intermittently operated and did not operate in 2024.

The WVDP also has EPA approval for radiological releases from up to 15 portable ventilation units (PVUs) at one time. Locations
of PVUs may change throughout the year depending on operational needs. Eight PVUs were active during 2024.

Radiological air emissions are regulated by the EPA NESHAP, which specifies that total annual airborne emissions from WVDP
sources shall not exceed a dose of 10 mrem/year to any member of the public. Exhaust from each EPA-approved ventilation
system at the WVDP is continuously filtered before being released to the atmosphere. Emissions are sampled for radioactivity
in both particulate (e.g., cesium-137, strontium-90, and plutonium-239/240) and gaseous forms (e.g., iodine-129) at the build-
ing stacks, PVUs, and off-site ambient air samplers. Iodine-129 is not sampled and analyzed for at the PVUs. The doses from
radiological air releases are evaluated and reported to the EPA in the annual NESHAP report and are discussed in Chapter 5,
“Dose Assessment“ in this document.

Possible diffuse emissions from deconstruction activities are evaluated before deconstruction work is performed based on the
measured source terms (radioisotope inventory) within a structure using calculation methods dictated in the NESHAP regu-
lations. Emissions during deconstruction activities on site are measured by the off-site ambient air monitoring network. (See
Figure A-7 in Appendix A.)

Diffuse source emissions. Diffuse emissions to air from surface water evaporation off the on-site lagoons are estimated each
year based on the annual average concentrations of radioisotopes in the lagoons. Radioisotope concentrations in the lagoons
in 2024 were similar to those in previous years.

Nonradiological Air Emissions. The WVDP maintains an Air Facility Registration Certificate and routinely evaluates new po-
tential sources of nonradiological emissions to determine if any new sources need to be added to the registration, or any new
monitoring or permitting is required. The only emissions currently covered under the Air Facility Registration Certificate are fu-
gitive emissions from the use of spray foam sealants, which are far below the permitting threshold. The site’s diesel and natural
gas emergency generators and fire pumps are operated less than 500 hours per year and thus are exempt from permitting. Only
low-sulfur diesel fuel is used on site. All asbestos removal activities are routinely monitored for asbestos emissions. No other
nonradiological monitoring or nonradiological air emissions permits are required at the WVDP.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 4-3

Meteorological Monitoring

The on-site meteorological tower continuously monitors wind speed, wind direction, and temperature at the 33-ft (10-m) and
197­-ft (60-m) elevations. (See Figure A-1 for the location.) The meteorological tower sends data to the digital and analog data
acquisition system on site. The systems are provided with backup power in the event of site power failures. Documentation,
such as meteorological system calibration records, site log books, and analog strip charts, are stored in protected archives.

In 2024, the data recovery rate (the percentage of valid recorded data per sampling period) was 94.0%. This meteorological
monitoring at the WVDP provides representative and verifiable data that characterize the local climatology. These data are
used to assess the potential effects of routine and nonroutine releases of airborne radioactivity and to provide input data to
airborne dispersion models (e.g., CAP88-PC), which can be used to calculate doses to off-site residents. These data can also be
used by the Emergency Response Organization (ERO) at the WVDP to predict the direction of plume migration if an unplanned
air release occurs.

 On-Site Air Monitoring in Deconstruction Work Areas
 On-site deconstruction work and radioactivity in the air are monitored on cameras and digital displays in an on-site control
 room in real time. When an anomaly is detected, the finding is promptly communicated to on-site operational personnel.
 Additionally, layers of air monitoring samplers are installed close to the work area to protect the health and safety of workers
 during the deconstruction of radiologically contaminated facilities. These work area samplers provide early indication of un-
 expected elevated readings to allow action to be taken, such as pausing deconstruction operations, before the site workers
 and the public are exposed to risk.

 Air monitors and samplers like the ones shown below are used at the WVDP site. Continuous Air Monitors (CAMs) placed
 near the deconstruction work area detect elevated levels of radioactivity and provide an early warning should adjustments
 to deconstruction activities be necessary. Radiation Protection personnel have continuous, direct access to the CAM readings
 from the operations control room. The Fixed Air Samplers (FASs) are routinely analyzed by the on-site laboratory during all
 worker activities. Radiation Protection personnel also survey designated surfaces and particle deposition plates for radioac-
 tivity many times daily during deconstruction and waste management activities.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 4-5

The prevalent wind directions measured in 2024 at the meteorological tower are shown by the wind roses on Figure 4-2 on the previ-
ous page. In 2024, the average wind speed at the 33-ft (10-m) elevation was 2.02 m/s (4.52 mph), and the average wind speed at the
197­-ft (60-m) elevation was 3.37 m/s (7.53 mph). The wind direction is influenced by the topography around the site. The
WVDP is located in a northwest-southeast trending valley as evidenced by the direction of the prevailing wind at the site. Total
precipitation in 2024 was 43.96 inches (112 cm), very close to the 43.54 inches (110 cm) 10-year annual average. (See Table 4-1.
on the previous page) Site barometric pressure is also measured on the meteorological tower at ground level.

On-Site Airborne Emissions Monitoring Update for 2024
The deconstruction of the MPPB began in September 2022 and the emissions from the deconstruction activities continued to
be monitored closely throughout 2024. Tables C-1 to C-4 in Appendix C presents total radioactivity released for specific radionu-
clides at each of the on-site air emission point sources. The on-site air emission concentrations from the stacks were compared
to the background sampling results in Great Valley, NY (location code AFGRVAL), approximately 18 miles (29 km) south of the
site. (See Figure A-14 for the background location.) This background location has sampled regional air with very low potential
to be affected by radiological releases from the WVDP for decades and provides a good indicator of background concentrations
at the WVDP as well. Gross alpha and gross beta results from 2024 at AFGRVAL are presented in Table C-5.

The background comparison results, including on-site and off-site air, surface water, food, and the TLD measurements, are sum-
marized in Table 4-2 on pages 4-23 and 4-24. In 2024, no radiological constituents measured at ANSTSTK and ANRHWFK were
statistically higher than background measurements. (Please refer to Chapter 8, “Radiation and Environmental Standards” for
more details about background comparison.)

T&VDS emissions (ANSTSTK): The concentrations measured in 2024 from the STS stack that monitors the T&VDS were in the
historical range; average concentrations of radioisotopes were several orders of magnitudes lower than the DCSs (Table C-1).

RHWF emissions (ANRHWFK): Remote size-reduction to segregate and repackage waste continued in the RHWF throughout
2024. The monitoring results in Table C-2 show that there were only very low levels of radioactivity emissions from this unit in
2024, all well below the DCSs.

CSPF emissions (ANCSPFK): No container sorting and packaging have been performed in the CSPF since 2021.

PVU emissions: PVUs were used in eight locations during 2024, the majority of which were inside the MPPB. The sum of the
emissions from all of the PVUs used in 2024 was well below the DCSs, as seen in Table C-4.

The levels of diffuse source emissions from lagoons in 2024 were
similar to the results in the previous years. (See Chapter 5 for
estimated annual doses to the public.)

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 4-7

The high-volume sampler (AF16HNNW) operates at a flow
rate more than five times that of the other low-volume
samplers and was installed to confirm the results of the lower
volume sampling. The low-volume sampling system is able to
detect radioisotope concentrations to approximately 1% of
each radioisotope’s environmental regulatory compliance
level.

The high-volume sampler can detect particulate radioisotopes
down to approximately 0.1% to 0.2% of the compliance
level. (Although the high-volume sampler does not include
a sample for iodine-129, the co-located low-volume sampler
does measure iodine-129.)

Off-site filter samples are collected biweekly for gross
alpha and gross beta screening and charcoal cartridges are Ambient air sampler in the north-northwest direction
collected monthly for iodine-129 screening analysis. These (photo credit: S. Wedvik)
samples, collected on a biweekly or monthly basis, go
through an on-site screening to promptly detect any anomaly. Then the biweekly filters are composited quarterly and sent to an
analytical laboratory for detailed isotopic analysis. The isotopes tested are strontium-90, iodine-129, cesium-137, uranium-232,
plutonium-238, plutonium-239/240, and americium-241, which are radioisotopes that were determined to have existed on the
site when this monitoring program was designed. The same tests are performed on samples collected at a background ambient
air sampler located in Great Valley. Both off-site and background samples include naturally occurring radioisotopes such as
radon decay products that are detected in gross alpha and beta measurements.

The off-site ambient air samplers are visited once a week for inspection and maintenance. A remote power surveillance system
monitors the power at the ambient air sampling stations. When power is lost, such as during a storm or when components
malfunction, the system automatically sends a text message to the Environmental Services (ES) air sampling technician, who
will quickly check and repair the sampling system.

The ability to immediately respond to power outages minimizes down-time, helping to ensure the ambient air surrounding the
site is sampled in all sectors continuously. However, power outages could result in the loss of up to a week of air monitoring
data, particularly after a severe storm. The ambient air samplers ran 98.7% of the year in 2024.

Off-Site Ambient Air Monitoring Update for 2024
The radioisotopic data from the ambient air samplers are used to demonstrate compliance with EPA air emissions standards for
exposure to the public and are compared with the NESHAP concentration levels for environmental compliance.

During 2024, very low levels of several isotopes were positively detected at the ambient air monitoring samplers, primarily in
the prevailing wind directions. Annual average americium-241, cesium-137, and strontium-90 at AF14 WNW (west northwest)
statistically exceeded the annual averages at the background air monitoring location in Great Valley. However, annual averages
of quarterly samples through the full year showed that the concentrations at all ambient air monitoring locations were far
below the NESHAP compliance concentrations.

Off-site air monitoring data in 2024 are summarized in Tables C-5 and C-6 in Appendix C.

The estimated total effective dose equivalents (EDEs) from the isotopic concentrations on the ambient air samplers for the
maximally exposed off-site individual (MEOSI) and the population in a 50-mile (80-km) radius from the site and are reported in
Chapter 5.

4-8 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

Surface Water Monitoring Program
The WVDP site is drained by several small streams flowing off site. Franks Creek enters from the south and receives drainage
from the south plateau. As it flows northward, Franks Creek is joined by Erdman Brook, which receives effluent from the LLW
treatment building (LLW2) through the lagoon system. After leaving the site at the security fence, Franks Creek receives drain-
age from the northeast swamp areas on the north plateau and from Quarry Creek, which receives drainage from the north
swamp location, WNSW74A. (See Figure 4-4 for the location.) Franks Creek then flows into Buttermilk Creek, which, after flow-
ing northward through the WNYNSC, enters Cattaraugus Creek and flows westward away from the WNYNSC. Cattaraugus Creek
ultimately drains into Lake Erie to the northwest. (See also Figure A-14.)

The primary releases from the site to surface waters occur at three locations, the lagoon 3 weir which discharges at outfall 001
([sampling location code WNSP001] on Figure 4-4), the northeast swamp drainage ditch (WNSWAMP) by natural drainage, and
the north swamp drainage ditch (WNSW74A), also by natural drainage.

Members of the public do not have access to the WVDP premises and therefore do not have any potential of direct exposure to
surface water on site. The first point of public access to surface water potentially impacted by the site is on Cattaraugus Creek
downstream of the WVDP at Felton Bridge (location code WFFELBR) shown on Figure 4-8 on page 4-17.

State Pollutant Discharge Elimination System (SPDES) Permit Required Monitoring
Liquid discharges from the WVDP site are regulated under a NYS SPDES permit. The permit lists compliance points from which
liquid effluents are released to Erdman Brook and specifies the sampling and analytical requirements for each. The conditions
and requirements of the current SPDES permit include monitoring of four wastewater discharge outfalls (only one of which,
outfall 001, is an active discharge point) and 19 stormwater discharge outfalls.

Stormwater. Rain and snow-melt events flowing over land or impervious surfaces (e.g., paved streets, parking lots, and build-
ing rooftops) generate stormwater runoff. The runoff flows on the surface, potentially transferring pollutants to surface water,
groundwater, and soil. Runoff can also move surface soils and sediment downgradient, which can lead to increased surface soil
erosion and sedimentation in creeks.

Requirements of the SPDES permit for monitoring stormwater runoff include water quality assessment at specific stormwater
discharge locations. (See Figures A-3 and A-4 for the outfall locations.) The amount of rainfall, the storm event duration, vol-
ume discharged, and the maximum flow rate must also be reported at each stormwater outfall. The WVDP stormwater outfalls
are grouped into eight representative drainage basins that could potentially be influenced by industrial, construction, or de-
construction activity runoff. One representative outfall from each group must be sampled on a semiannual basis. Stormwater
samples are not required to be analyzed for radiological parameters under the SPDES permit but are screened for gross alpha
and gross beta radioactivity at the WVDP on-site laboratory. The stormwater sampling results are provided in Appendix B-3.

The original SPDES Discharge Monitoring Reports (DMRs) are posted on the WVCA website (https://www.westvalleycleanup.
com/).

4-10 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 Radiological Data Evaluation
  Derived Concentration Standards (DCSs). “DCSs are radiological quantities, used in the development and conduct of
  environmental and public radiation protection programs implemented at DOE sites and for DOE activities. A DCS for
  exposure to a radionuclide in a specified environmental medium (e.g., air or water) represents the concentration of
  the radionuclide in that medium that would result in an effective dose of 1 millisievert (mSv) [100 millirem (mrem)]
  to a reference member of the population based on continuous exposure for one year (e.g., the concentration in water
  consumed over a year that would cause 1 mSv).” as defined in DOE-STD-1196-2022. The intended applications of DCSs
  are as follows:

  • Defining criteria for applying Best Available Technology (BAT) at the point of discharge for liquid effluent streams (in
 accordance with DOE Order 458.1, “Radiation Protection of the Public and the Environment.”)

  • Relative ranking of the importance of radionuclides within a waste stream.

  • Relative ranking of multiple effluent streams to air or water.

  The DCSs were developed with consideration of only three exposure modes (ingestion of water, inhalation of air, and air
  submersion). In 2021, ingestion of milk was added to these exposure modes. While they provide relative guidance for the
  ranking of potential radionuclides in effluent streams released from facilities, they are not intended to be used to infer
  the dose to members of the public nor to demonstrate compliance with DOE radiation protection dose limits. “The DCS
  values represent concentrations at the point of discharge and do not account for attenuation along the pathway before
  reaching the receptor. Typically, more complex environmental pathways are involved. A detailed pathway analysis is
  required for calculating public radiation doses resulting from DOE activities.“ (DOE-STD-1196-2022).

  DCSs applicable to the radionuclides present at the WVDP are presented in Table 8-4 in Chapter 8, “Radiation and
  Environmental Standards” of this report. When only gross alpha and beta measurements are available in WVDP air sample
  results, activity is assumed to come from plutonium-239/240 and strontium-90, respectively, because the DCSs for these
  radionuclides are the most limiting for major WVDP particulate emissions. For water effluents, when only gross alpha and
  beta measurements are available, activity is assumed to come from uranium-232 for gross alpha, and strontium-90 for
  gross beta, also because their DCSs are the most limiting for major WVDP waterborne exposures.

  Sum of Ratios. Environmental sampling results at the WVDP are assessed to determine whether the constituents of
  interest are present and, if so, their concentrations are compared with DCSs as guidelines for controlling potential
  exposure to the public. To evaluate the radioactivity released from each location with respect to the DCSs, the annual
  average radionuclide concentration measured for each nuclide was divided by its respective DCS and the ratios from all
  nuclides are summed. If the sum of the ratios (also called the “sum of fractions”) exceeds 1.0, or if expressed as the sum
  of percentages, exceeds 100%, then the total radioactivity released from that location during the current year exceeds
  DCSs and further evaluation is required.

  This comparison to DCSs is a very conservative method of evaluating the concentration data because it assumes a
  continuous exposure by drinking the water or inhaling the air, at this concentration, at the point of measurement, which
  may be at an on-site location where no public exposure is possible and where no water is ingested. DCSs are therefore
  used as a precaution to ensure releases/emissions are not approaching levels that could cause dose limits to be exceeded
  off site. The regulatory limit is based on the modeled/measured total annual off-site dose, not this comparison to DCSs.

  Statistical Comparison to Background. Data from near-site locations are compared with background concentrations using
  standard statistical methods to assess possible site impacts on the environment. Refer to Chapter 7, “Quality Assurance”,
  and Chapter 8, “Radiation and Environmental Standards” for more discussions on measurement result analysis. Results
  from each location are also compared to the current year and/or historical data from the backgound locations to
  determine if any trends (e.g., increasing constituent concentrations) are occurring. (See Table 4-2.)

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 4-11

Whole Effluent Toxicity (WET) Testing. The SPDES permit also includes periodic special studies, such as discharge effluent
toxicity testing every five years. This test involves sending samples of the SPDES discharge waters from the site to a bioassay
laboratory where vertebrate (fathead minnow) and invertebrate (water flea) freshwater species are tested and evaluated for
survival rate, growth rates, and rates of reproduction. The Chapter 2, “Environmental Compliance Summary” describes the
WET testing for 2024.

Stormwater Discharge Compliance. The DOE DCSs are used for reviewing the compliance of radioisotope concentrations in dis-
charge and surface water. Regulatory limits for chemical constituents in discharges to surface water under the SPDES program,
and additional water quality and potable water standards are listed in Appendix B-1.

Waterborne Radiological Releases. Controlled SPDES discharges from outfall 001 contain radioactivity and must be pre-
approved for release by NYSDEC. Pre-discharge radiological data are compared to DOE DCSs and provided to NYSDEC to facili-
tate this approval, as required by the SPDES permit.

Radiological Data Evaluation. The curies released from the discharge points are summarized in Appendix B-2A, B-4A and
B-4B for the three on-site discharge points. Annual curies at outfall 001 (WNSP001) and WNSWAMP are summarized using
flow-weighted mean concentrations (FWMCs) because of the batch nature of discharges from outfall 001 and elevated isotope
concentrations at WNSWAMP (See the inset box “Calculating Flow-Weighted Mean Concentrations” below.) The annual aver-
age for each radioisotope at each location is compared to the DCS to determine a ratio. These ratios are then summed. A sum
of ratios less than 1.0 indicates that the annual average concentration from the water discharged from this location was less
than DCSs.

2024 Update for SPDES Required Monitoring
SPDES Outfall Results. There were no SPDES effluent limit exceedances and no SPDES noncompliance events during 2024. The
2024 toxicity testing results are reported in Table B-2K in Appendix B-2.

Outfall 001 (WNSP001). During MPPB deconstruction, water used for dust suppression is captured by the site water manage-
ment system. This water is evaluated to determine if it can be treated in the LLW treatment system and discharged through
outfall 001 or requires off-site disposal. Three batch releases totaling approximately 5.8 million gallons (22.1 million L) were
discharged from outfall 001 in 2024, which was about 1.7 times the discharge volume in 2023. Usually, three or four discharges
from outfall 001 are conducted in a year. The sum of isotope concentration/DCS ratios for the release from outfall 001 in 2024
was 0.25 (or 25% of the DCSs as shown in Table B-2A. The sum of the ratios at this location has continued to decrease over the
years.

The isotopic distribution in discharge from 001 was similar to recent years in mean concentrations. The largest contributor to
the sum of ratios at outfall 001 in 2024 was strontium-90 (0.23 of the 0.25 total). The ratio of strontium-90 was 0.27 out of the
total ratio of 0.29 in 2023. For other isotopes, the ratio of cesium-137 increased and the ratio of uranium-232 decreased com-
pared with last year, however, both changes were minor (a change of approximately 1.00E-03 or less.)

 Calculating Flow-Weighted Mean Concentrations
 Flow-weighted mean concentrations (FWMC) are concentrations that are adjusted for the variability in stream flow
 over a given period of time (e.g., monthly or annually). FWMC is useful for estimating the typical concentration of
 a contaminant adjusting for stream flow. This allows for comparisons between streams with different flow volumes
 between years.

 Flow-weighted mean concentration is defined as = Total Load (kg or Ci)
 Total Stream Flow Volume (m3)

 where the total load (kg or Ci) is divided by the total stream volume (m3) for a given time period (e.g., year or month). By
 calculating FWMC on a monthly or annual basis, variability due to seasonal and historical sampling frequency fluctuations
 and missing data can be reduced.

4-12 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

On-Site Liquid Effluent/Surface Water Monitoring
Natural surface water drainage to Franks Creek from WNSWAMP largely consists of emergent groundwater supplemented
by surface water runoff. (See Figure 4-4 for the location.) Elevated gross beta concentrations in surface water were first mea-
sured at WNSWAMP in 1993. Subsequent investigations delineated a plume of strontium-90-contaminated groundwater on
the north plateau that discharges to the surface water flowing through the WNSWAMP location (See Chapter 6, “Groundwater
Protection Program” for detail about the groundwater gross beta/strontium-90 plume.) Strontium-90 contaminated surface
water from the WNSWAMP drainage ditch flows into Franks Creek, then into Buttermilk Creek, and ultimately into Cattaraugus
Creek, where it is sampled at Felton Bridge (WFFELBR), the first point of public access.

Natural surface water drainage to Quarry Creek is sampled at WNSW74A (Figure 4-4). Concentrations of radionuclides are gen-
erally lower at this location than at WNSWAMP. Surface water from this drainage has historically contributed a small fraction
of the total waterborne dose from the site.

Potential Surface Water Contamination Sources on the North Plateau. On the north plateau, possible contaminant sources,
other than the planned discharges at outfall 001 and the groundwater plume, to surface water include the Waste Tank Farm
(WTF), MPPB deconstruction activities, the lagoon system associated with the LLW2, and waste handling and storage facil-
ities. North plateau surface water sampling locations that monitor these potential sources include locations WNSP005 and
WNSP006, in addition to WNSWAMP and WNSW74A.

Potential Surface Water Contamination Sources on the South Plateau. On the south plateau, the two inactive underground
radioactive waste disposal areas (the NRC-licensed disposal area [NDA] and NYS-licensed disposal area [SDA]), the 56 Vertical
Storage Casks (VSCs) (stored on the interim HLW Cask Storage Pad), the drum cell (a building formerly used to store drums of
processed LLW), and waste management activities are all potential, although not anticipated sources of contamination.

Surface water drainage across the south plateau is monitored at locations WNNDADR, WNERB53, and WNFRC67 (Figure 4-4).
Drainage is directed around the NDA and SDA by stormwater drainage pipes, culverts, and drop inlets.

The on-site surface water drainage is routinely sampled for pH and radiological parameters at several other points on the north
and south plateaus. These monitoring points are sited at locations where releases from potential source areas on the north and
south plateaus could be detected. This vigilance allows site operations to be modified as needed if anomalous concentrations
are detected in the surface water.

Environmental Compliance Limits for Potential Surface Water Contamination. The regulatory limit for radioactivity in water-
borne releases from DOE facilities is the dose limit of 100 mrem/year (1 millisievert [mSv]/year) to an off-site individual from
all pathways, per DOE Order 458.1.

2024 Update for Liquid Effluent/Surface Water
WNSWAMP. Natural drainage flow through the WNSWAMP location in 2024 was approximately 12.4 million gallons [gal] (46.9
million liters [L]). The sum of ratios of radioisotopes discharged from WNSWAMP against the DCSs was 0.25 (or 25% of DCSs)
in 2024, as shown in Table B-4A in Appendix B-4. The sum of ratios was lower than the 2023 sum of 0.31 (or 31%). As in past
years, the sum of ratios at WNSWAMP was almost entirely attributable to strontium-90. In 2024, gross alpha, gross beta, and
strontium-90 measurements at WNSWAMP statistically exceeded background measurements at the upstream sampling loca-
tion on Buttermilk Creek (location code WFBCBKG on Figure A-5 in Appendix A). (See Table 4-2.) However, the strontium-90
concentration remained below the DCS.

WNSW74A. Natural drainage flow through the WNSW74A location in 2024 was estimated to be 12.3 million gal (46.7 million
L), similar to the volume in 2023. The sum of ratios of radioisotopes discharged from WNSW74A was 0.023 (or 2.3% of DCSs),
identical to the sum in 2023, and was less than one-tenth of that observed at WNSWAMP. (See Table B-4B on Appendix B-4.)

The majority of the radioactivity at WNSW74A in 2024 was attributable to strontium-90 (0.021 in the ratio), as it has been
historically. In 2024, gross alpha, gross beta, and strontium-90 results at WNSW74A statistically exceeded the background mea-
surements; however, the results were more than an order of magnitude below the DCS values.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 4-13

A historical comparison of the annual average strontium-90 concentrations from WNSWAMP and WNSW74A is shown in
Figure 4-5 below. As seen in this figure, the concentrations observed at WNSW74A that flows into Quarry Creek are much
lower than the concentrations at WNSWAMP, which flows into Franks Creek. Gross beta increased slightly around 2010 due to
a change in the site water management system. The change lowered the volume of clean surface water flowing through the
ditches which led to less diluted water flow and increased concentrations of strontium-90. The concentrations began decreas-
ing again afterward with the reduction of strontium-90 in groundwater toward these directions. (See Chapter 6, “Groundwater
Protection Program”, for more details of the groundwater strontium-90 plume.)

North Plateau (WNSP005 and WNSP006). WNSP005 is located east of the MPPB, downgradient of former lagoon 1 and other
areas of localized contamination (e.g., Solvent Dike). (Lagoon 1 was one of the five open lagoons in the LLW2 area and was
contaminated with tritium. Lagoon 1 was taken out of service, backfilled, and capped in the 1980s.) Although the flow volume
through the WNSP005 drainage ditch is very small, these areas of localized contamination impact the groundwater that dis-
charges into the drainage ditch leading to WNSP005.

In 2024, five radiological isotopes were analyzed at WNSP005. Gross alpha, gross beta, and strontium-90 were positively de-
tected at WNSP005 in 2024 based on the annual average concentrations. (See Table B-4C.) And those concentrations exceeded
the background measurements. The gross beta annual average exceeded the DCS standard by about 31%. The concentration of
gross alpha was approximately an order of magnitude below the DCS (DCS of uranium-232), and the strontium-90 concentration
was slightly less than the DCS value.

WNSP006 discharges site surface water to Franks Creek on the eastern edge of the north plateau. As in previous years, con-
centrations at WNSP006 for gross alpha/beta and strontium-90 in 2024 were lower than those at WNSP005, well below DCSs.
Sixteen analytes were tested at this drainage location. (See Table B-4D.) In 2024, gross beta and strontium-90 statistically ex-
ceeded background.

and 82%, and the strontium-90 concentrations have decreased by 96% and 90% respectively, since 2008. The cap and
slurry wall surrounding the NDA have effectively reduced surface water infiltration and groundwater migration through the

2024 Update for Off-Site Stream Monitoring
Felton Bridge (WFFELBR). Sampling point WFFELBR is a favorite launch point for kayakers. The composite samples compiled
monthly at WFFELBR in 2024. The 2024 monitoring results are tabulated in Table B-4I. Only gross beta exceeded the historical
background measurements at WFBIGBR in 2024. Gross beta is frequently detected in clean areas due to minor amounts of
natural gross beta (geological source) in the sediment entrained in the creek water samples.

The maximum gross alpha concentration seen in 2024 at WFFELBR was 0.82% of the uranium-232 DCS guidance value. The
maximum beta concentration was 0.42% of the DCS, and the maximum strontium-90 concentration was only 0.05% of the DCS.
