stream of the WVDP. The composite samples are collected semiannually at this location. In 2024, gross beta collected at
WFBCTCB statistically exceeded the background concentrations on Buttermilk Creek (WFBCBKG) as in previous years. As stat-
ed above, gross beta is largely naturally occurring and is typically detected in samples both upstream and downstream of the
WVDP. The maximum gross beta concentration at WFBCTCB was 0.53% of the strontium-90 DCS. (2024 data are summarized
in Table B-4H.)

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 4-17

Sediment and Soil Monitoring Program
Airborne particulates may be deposited onto soil by wind or precipitation. Particulate matter in streams can adsorb radiological
constituents in liquid effluents and settle on the stream bottom as sediment. Soils and sediment may subsequently be eroded or
resuspended, especially during periods of high winds or high stream flow. Those soil particles provide a pathway for radiological
constituents to reach humans either directly via exposure or indirectly through the food and water ingestion pathway.

Soil and sediment samples were last collected in 2022 and are scheduled for their next collection in 2027 as part of the five-
year schedule. On-site sediment/soil samples were collected at three locations on the north plateau where drainage has the
potential to be contaminated. Those three locations are SNSP006, SNSWAMP, and SNSW74A. (See Figure A-2.) Soil samples were
also collected off-site at one background location (SFGRVAL, shown on Figure A-14) and at three off-site locations coinciding
with the off-site air sampling locations (SFRSPRD, SFFXVRD, and SFRT240, shown on Figure A-5). Additional off-site sediment
samples were collected at one background location on Buttermilk Creek (SFBCSED) and at three downstream locations: one on
Buttermilk Creek (SFTCSED) and two on Cattaraugus Creek (SFCCSED and SFSDSED). (See Figure A-5.)

Drinking Water Monitoring Program
The WVDP maintains a nontransient noncommunity drinking water supply that is used only by site employees, primarily for
showering. Project drinking water (potable water) and industrial water have been supplied by two bedrock wells since 2014,
and the wells are monitored as part of the source water protection and drinking water monitoring plans. Additionally, bottled
water is provided to site employees for consumption. Supplemental water needed for SPDES flow augmentation water contin-
ues to be supplied by the lakes south of the MPPB.

Drinking water continues to be monitored for both radiological and chemical constituents. Monitoring is conducted at the dis-
tribution entry point and at several site tap water locations to verify compliance with EPA, NYSDOH, and Cattaraugus County
Health Department (CCHD) regulations. In 2021, the WVDP began monitoring per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) com-
pounds for the site drinking water per CCHD requirements.

2024 Drinking Water Update
Monitoring results from 2024 indicated that the WVDP site’s drinking water continued to remain below the local, state, and
federal MCLs and drinking water standards for chemical contaminants. Radiological measurements for the two supply wells
and the nearby bedrock wells were similar to previous years, a few orders of magnitude smaller than the DCSs. The sampling
results for the potable water supply system and sentinel well water are presented in Appendix B-5.

In 2024, according to the updated EPA guidance on PFAS compounds in the drinking water supply (described in the inset on
the next page,) one drinking water supply well on the WVDP premises was sampled according to the sampling schedule set
by the CCHD. The results of six PFAS compounds as well as 1,4-dioxane with individual MCLs, are reported in Tables B-5I and
B-5J. Additionally, a hazard index was calculated for PFAS mixtures containing at least two or more of PFHxS, PFNA, HFPO-DA,
and PFBS. (See Table B-5J.) The sampling results were all nondetects, and the hazard index was 0.2, less than the MCL violation
threshold of 1.

Food Source Monitoring Program
Food samples are collected from locations near the site and from remote locations for comparison purposes. The near-site
samples are collected at the locations where plants and animals could potentially be impacted by site activities. (See Figure
A-11.) Background samples are collected far from the site at the locations where no contamination from the site could occur.
(See Figure A-14.)

Corn, apples, and beans are collected at harvest time (>1 kg or 2.2 lb). Venison (deer meat) samples are typically collected
during the fall when deer are most active, and fish may be collected at any time of the year but are not usually collected during
the winter. Only the edible portions of the deer and fish are sampled and analyzed for radionuclides. Milk and venison (deer)
samples are collected every year and fish, apples, beans, and corn are collected every five years. They were last sampled in
2022.

4-18 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 Per- and Polyfluoroalkyl Substances
 (PFAS) Sampling
 Background
 A group of man-made chemicals known as per- and
 polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) were first manufactured in
 the 1940s. PFAS include chemicals such as perfluorooctanoic
 acid (PFOA), which is used to make coatings for items such
 as water-repellent clothing and heat-resistant nonstick
 cookware. Another compound, perfluorooctane sulfonate
 (PFOS), was used in fire-fighting foam. The reason why
 PFAS are known as “forever chemicals” is that they do not
 breakdown easily and bioaccumulate over time. PFAS have
 been detected in surface water and groundwater across
 the country and are linked to cancer, thyroid disease, and
 other health ailments. The EPA designated PFAS as one of
 the emerging contaminants of concern in 2016, and the
 Agency for Toxic Substances and Disease Registry (ATSDR)
 designated PFAS as a public health concern.
 DOE Actions
 “Pre- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS)”
                                                                                       (DOE. 2023)
 Following the EPA designation, the DOE, led by the Office of
 Environment, Health, Safety, and Security (EHSS), conducted its initial assessment of the PFAS presence at the DOE sites,
 including nuclear sites. An initial historical site information survey at the WVDP suggests that PFOA, PFOS, or other PFAS
 substances were not used or produced at the WVDP. The results of the initial assessment can be seen at https://shorturl.
 at/JDqhZ.

 In December 2024, DOE memo, “Managing Emerging Environmental Issues at the Department of Energy,” was signed and
 relieved the DOE of its responsibility to define PFAS-bearing wastes. However, the DOE sites continue to follow applicable
 state and federal regulations pertaining to PFAS storage and disposal. DOE sites are advised to continue their efforts to
 eliminate the use of PFAS-containing products and to minimize environmental releases of PFAS substances.

 EPA Final Ruling
 The WVDP began sampling and analysis for PFOA, PFOS, and 1,4-dioxane in January 2021 under the direction of the
 Cattaraugus County Health Department (CCHD), New York. In April 2024, the EPA established legally enforceable levels,
 called Maximum Contaminant Levels (MCLs), for six categories of PFAS in drinking water: PFOA, PFOS, PFHxS, PFNA, and
 HFPO-DA as contaminants with individual MCLs, and PFAS mixtures containing at least two or more of PFHxS, PFNA, HFPO-
 DA, and PFBS using a Hazard Index MCL to account for the combined and co-occurring levels of these PFAS in drinking water.

 New York State Actions

 In August 2020, NYSDOH adopted a drinking water MCL of 10 parts per trillion (ppt) for PFOA and PFOS and 1.0 parts per
 billion (ppb) for 1,4-dioxane and issued requirements that water supply systems begin sampling for these constituents.
 To ensure consistency in sampling, analysis, reporting, and assessment of PFAS, the NYSDEC’s Division of Environmental
 Remediation (DER) has developed a sampling and analysis guidance document that summarizes currently accepted
 procedures to be used for all PFAS sampling plans submitted to NYSDEC under 6 NYCRR Part 375 remedial programs.
 (“Sampling, Analysis, and Assessment of Per and Polyfluoroalkyl Substances (PFAS) Under NYSDEC’s Part 375 Remedial
 Programs” [April 2023]) (https://www.dec.ny.gov/docs/remediation_hudson_pdf/pfassampanaly.pdf).

 (continued on page 4-20)

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 4-19

  (continued from page 4-19)

  PFAS Sample Analysis
  Currently, more than a few PFAS analysis methods exist, and each method is tailored for a particular matrix (media),
  utilizing a specific analysis technology. The PFAS samples collected at the WVDP are analyzed using the method, EPA
  533, for drinking water at an EPA 533-certified analytical laboratory. The EPA 533 utilizes analytical techniques such as
  isotope dilution, anion exchange, solid phase extraction (SPE), and liquid chromatography/tandem mass spectrometry.
  The laboratory analyzed drinking water for 25 PFAS substances and PFAS mixtures in 2024. For more information about the
  status of PFAS regulations and sampling, please refer to the websites below.

  EPA
  https://www.epa.gov/pfas
  https://www.epa.gov/sdwa/and-polyfluoroalkyl-substances-pfas

  DOE
  https://www.energy.gov/pfas/pfas-and-polyfluoroalkyl-substances
  https://extapps.dec.ny.gov/docs/remediation_hudson_pdf/pfassampanaly.pdf

  New York State
  https://dec.ny.gov/environmental-protection/site-cleanup/pfas

2024 Food Monitoring Update
A low level of cesium-137 was detected in one of the venison background samples in 2024. Also, a low level of strontium-90
was detected in one of the near-site venison samples. However, the concentration levels of both positive detections were in
the ranges of historical measurements of background venisons and were not statistically different from these. Tritium was not
detected in any venison (background or near-site) in 2024. Because of the fallout from nuclear weapons tests and from nuclear
accidents, cesium-137 and strontium-90 could be found in the bodies of wild animals through ingesting wild plants, and positive
detections of these isotopes are not unexpected (Savannah River Ecology Laboratory 1999).

Neither cesium-137, strontium-90, nor tritium was detected in a near-site milk sample (location code BFMSCHT on Figure A-11).
Naturally occurring potassium-40 is nearly always detected in milk and was detected in the near-site milk sample this year as
well. Measurement data from 2024 for milk and venison samples are provided in Appendix E.

 Milk samples are collected annually at near-site Venison samples are collected annually from deer near
 and off-site dairy farms the site (photo credit: M. Regan)

4-20 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

Direct Radiation Monitoring
Thermoluminescent Dosimeters (TLDs) directly measure radiation (mostly gamma and beta radiation) in the environment. On
site, TLDs are placed at selected solid waste management units (SWMUs) and on the WVDP security fence. Off site, TLDs are
placed around the WNYNSC perimeter, the access road, and in Great Valley, the background sampling location. On-site TLD
locations are shown on Figure A-12, and the locations of TLDs around the WNYNSC perimeter are shown on Figure A-13. The
location of the TLD in Great Valley is shown on Figure A-14. TLDs are collected and measured twice a year with the unit of mR
(milliroentgen per quarter). An average exposure rate in 2024 at the background location (TLD #23) in Great Valley was about
16 mR/quarter. No changes were made to the locations of TLDs this year.

Direct Radiation Update for 2024
Figure 4-9 presents the average annual exposure rates (the unit converted to microroentgen [µR] per hour) over the last
10 years at the WNYNSC perimeter and the background location in Great Valley. The average results at the WNYNSC perimeter
TLDs (TLDs #1-16 and 20) have always been slightly lower than the background TLD measurements (TLD #23) (Figure A-14).
It is assumed that TLD measurements are higher at the background location due to geological characteristics in the area such
as natural uranium, thorium, radon, etc. No particular temporal trends have been observed for either data groups except for a
slight increase in background measurements in 2019. All 2024 TLD data are presented in Appendix F in units of milliroentgen
(mR)/quarter.

In 2024, elevated exposure rates were observed on site at TLDs #24 (on the northern edge of the site fence), #35 (near the
Drum Cell), #38 (west side of the MPPB), and #40 (near the WTF); similar locations on the north plateau that have had elevated
rates in previous years. The annual averages at these locations were statistically above the background location (#23 in Great
Valley). The exposure rates at TLDs #24 and #35 were approximately twice to four times that of the background. However,
the exposure rates at these two locations decreased in 2024 compared with 2023 measurements. The exposure rates at #38
remained elevated in 2024 (143±17 mR/quarter and 322±32 mR/quarter). These exposure rates were identical to the 2023
readings when averaged. TLD #35 is located near the on-site rail line where the packaged wastes are staged prior to off-site
shipment. TLD #38 is located on the west side of the MPPB where packages with elevated radioactivity are staged before pack-
ing and off-site shipment. The exposure rates at these two locations are affected by waste management/shipping activities.

Environmental Monitoring Summary
The WVDP continued the open-air deconstruction of the MPPB throughout CY 2024. The WVDP’s environmental monitoring
programs maintained its effectiveness and detected even minor changes in the air, water and biota near the WVDP site. This
rigorous surveillance ensured that MPPB deconstruction activities would not adversely affect the nearby environment and
the public’s health. The concentrations found to be above background concentrations were far below the standards, often by
multiple orders of magnitudes. As of the end of 2024, the MPPB deconstruction was about 75% complete with no incidents
of accidental release above standards or guidance levels. The WVDP environmental monitoring will continue in 2025 with the
same level of diligence to complete the last steps of the MPPB deconstruction in early summer 2025.

4-24 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 CHAPTER 5
 DOSE ASSESSMENT

Total Dose from All Pathways. The 2024 total estimated dose from the WVDP activities to a maximally exposed off-site indi-
vidual (MEOSI) was 0.184 mrem (0.00184 mSv), 0.18% of the DOE’s annual public dose limit, 100 mrem, from all pathways in a
calendar year.

Dose from the Air Pathway. Annual air emissions of radionuclides are regulated by the EPA and limited to 10 mrem during a calendar
year at the MEOSI. The total estimated annual dose from the WVDP activities in 2024 was 0.18 mrem (0.0018 mSv), well below the
10 mrem annual limit. There were three radioisotopes measured at a sampler in the west-northwest direction that were sta-
tistically higher than the background location. However, the measured concentrations were very low compared with the EPA
standards. The total airborne collective population dose in 2024 was 0.069 person-rem (0.00069 person-Sv), far below the
DOE’s 100 person-rem guidance for the routine environmental surveillance.

Dose from the Water Pathway. The estimated dose from the surface water exposure pathway is evaluated against the DOE’s
total all pathway dose limit of 100 mrem per year. The total estimated dose from surface water releases from the WVDP site
in 2024 was 0.004 mrem (0.00004 mSv), a very small fraction of the 100 mrem DOE dose limit. The total waterborne collective
population dose in 2024 was 0.038 person-rem (0.00038 person-Sv). Groundwater is not considered an exposure pathway
because no off-site public water supplies are drawn from aquifers potentially affected by the WVDP premises.

Dose to Biota. The results of biota dose modeling for 2024 indicated that the plants and animals living on the WNYNSC prem-
ises are not being exposed to doses in excess of the DOE biota dose standard of 1 rad/day (radiation absorbed dose) for aquatic
animals and terrestrial plants, and 0.1 rad/day for riparian and terrestrial animals.

Minimizing Potential Dose to the Public and the Environment
DOE Order 458.1, “Radiation Protection of the Public and the Environment,” establishes requirements to protect the public
and the environment against undue risk from radiation. This order ensures DOE operations are conducted in a manner that
limits any potential radiation exposures to As Low As Reasonably Achievable (ALARA). ALARA principles advocate controlling
or managing exposures to as low as technical and practical considerations permit, and as far below the applicable limits of the
order as practicable. Deliberate efforts are made at every level of the work to minimize exposure time, maximize distance from
the potential source, and to utilize shielding whenever possible. ALARA radiological controls protect the worker and, as a result,
also protect the public and the environment.

Radiation Sources at the WVDP
Members of the public are routinely exposed to natural and artificial ionizing radiation that can be absorbed by living tissue. (See
the inset on page 5-4 for discussions of “Radiation Dose” and “Units of Dose Measurement.”) In 2006, a typical individual living
in the U.S. was estimated to receive an average annual effective dose equivalent (EDE) of about 620 mrem (6.2 mSv) (National
Council on Radiation Protection and Measurements [NCRP] Report 160, 2009). NCRP Report No. 184 (2019), an update of the
medical exposure section of NCRP Report No. 160, indicates there has been a 15-20% reduction in the non-therapeutic medical
radiation dose to the U.S. population in the decade between 2006 and 2016. This reduction effectively reduces the NCRP Report
No. 160 estimate to about 560 to 580 mrem (5.6 to 5.8 mSv). (See Figure 5-1 for dose distribution of 620 mrem.)

Of the typical radiation dose to a member of the public, approximately 310 mrem/year is from natural background sources,
including cosmic radiation (from outer space) and terrestrial radiation and radon (from the earth’s subsurface). The remainder
is derived from artificial sources, including consumer products and medical diagnostic procedures. (See Chapter 8, “Radiation

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 5-1

and Environmental Standards” of this report for discussions of ionizing radiation.) The estimated (all pathway) maximum indi-
vidual dose from the WVDP in 2024 was 0.184 mrem (0.00184 mSv). Figure 5-1 below shows the estimated dose from the
WVDP in 2024 compared with the average annual dose a U.S. resident receives from artificial and natural background sources.

Exposure Pathways

Human beings are exposed to natural radiation and artificial radiation sources through various exposure pathways. Various envi-
ronmental media act as pathways that transfer contaminants from a source to a receptor. Potential exposure pathways include:
inhalation of gases and particulates, ingestion of locally grown food products and game, and exposure to external penetrating
radiation emitted from contaminated materials, as shown in Figure 5-2.

Table 5-1 summarizes the potential exposure pathways from the WVDP site to the local off-site population and describes the
rationale for including or excluding each pathway when calculating dose from the WVDP.

Background Corrections
The WVDP uses the environmental monitoring measurement results returned from qualified analytical laboratories for statis-
tical analysis and dose calculations. The WVDP does not correct measurement results for the natural radioactivity. Also, the
results are not corrected for locational background radioactivity (e.g., the WVDP does not subtract off-site airborne radioactiv-
ity from on-site airborne activity.) Instead of applying background corrections, the WVDP conducts sample versus background
(control) comparisons and compares on-site and near-site results against those from off-site samples, which are not affected
by the WVDP activities.

Dose from Airborne Emissions
For details of the WVDP air monitoring program, please refer to Chapter 4, “Environmental Monitoring.” Please refer to Table
A-2 in Appendix A for a list of isotopes analyzed at the on-site stacks and on the off-site ambient air monitoring samplers.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 5-3

 Radiation Dose
  The energy released from a radionuclide is eventually deposited in matter encountered along
  the path of the radiation. The ionizing radiation energy absorbed by a unit mass of material is
  referred to as the absorbed dose. The absorbing material can be either inanimate matter or
  living tissue.

  Alpha particles are unable to penetrate the human body beyond the skin layer but leave a dense
  track of ionization in human cells as they travel through tissue and thus deliver the most dose, or harm, per unit path-
  length. Because alpha particles are not penetrating, they must be taken into the body by inhalation or ingestion to cause
  harm to internal organs. Beta and gamma radiation can penetrate the protective skin layer from the outside, resulting in
  exposure of the internal organs to radiation.

  Considering the different biological effects of different kinds of radiation, the absorbed dose is multiplied by a quality
  factor specific to the isotope to yield a unit called the dose equivalent. A radiation dose expressed as a dose equivalent,
  rather than as an absorbed dose, allows for the comparison of risks from different types of radiation exposure (e.g.,
  exposure to alpha radiation versus exposure to gamma radiation). For this reason, regulatory agencies limit the dose to
  individuals in terms of total dose equivalent. Refer to the Chapter 8, “Radiation and Environmental Standards” for further
  discussion of ionizing radiation.

 Units of Dose Measurement
  The unit for dose equivalent commonly used in the U.S. is the rem. The international unit of dose equivalent is the sievert
  (Sv), which is equal to 100 rem. The millirem (mrem) and millisievert (mSv), used more frequently to report the low dose
  equivalents encountered in environmental exposures, are equal to one-thousandth of a rem or sievert, respectively.

  The effective dose equivalent (EDE), also expressed in units of rem or Sv, provides a means of combining unequal organ
  and tissue doses into a single “effective” whole body dose that represents a comparable risk probability. The effective
  dose is a weighted sum of equivalent (organ-specific) doses to radiosensitive tissues, representing the relative contribu-
  tion of the different tissues to the total risk for the idealized case of uniform irradiation of the whole body (DOE STD-1196-
  2022). The probability that a given dose will result in the induction of a fatal cancer is referred to as the risk (‘cancer risk’)
  associated with that dose. In this document, for waterborne releases, the EDE is calculated by dosimetry models recom-
  mended by the International Commission on Radiological Protection (ICRP) in Publications 26, 30, 48, and 56 through 72
  (as stated in the GENII version 2 User’s Guide) and risk factors in Federal Guidance Report (FGR) 15 (2019). For airborne
  emissions, the EDE calculation is based on the ICRP Report Number 72 (1995) and risk factors in FGR 13 (2019). Nuclear
  decay data are based on the ICRP Publication 107 (2008).

  A collective population dose is expressed in units of person-rem or person-sievert because the individual doses are
  summed over the entire potentially exposed population (persons). The 50-mile (80-km) radius collective dose is the sum
  of all doses to all individual members of the public within 50 miles of the WVDP. Dividing the person-rem by the popula-
  tion gives an average person-rem per person.

 DOE Ionizing Dose Ranges Chart
  The DOE Office of Public Radiation Protection issued a chart that provides an “order of magnitude” reference for various
  levels of radiation exposure (December 2017). This comprehensive chart provides perspective on the estimated dose
  from the WVDP compared to other types of radiation dose familiar to the public, such as the dose used for cancer treat-
  ments. (See Figure 8-1 in the Chapter 8, “Radiation and Environmental Standards” of this report to view the chart.)

5-4 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

On-Site Stacks
The EPA regulates airborne radionuclide emissions under the Clean Air Act (CAA). The DOE facilities are subject to 40 CFR
61, Subpart H, National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP), which is part of the CCA and contains the
national standards for emissions of radionuclides other than radon from DOE facilities. The applicable standard is a maximum
of 10 mrem (0.1 mSv) EDE to any member of the public in any year.

The number of point-sources (e.g., stacks) at the WVDP has decreased in recent years. Two stacks were active during 2024, the
supernatant Treatment System stack (ANSTSTK), which ventilates the HLW tanks, and the Remote Handled Waste Facility stack
(ANRHWF). Eight PVUs were used in select work areas to ventilate the MPPB during 2024 decommissioning activities. In recent
years, diffuse sources, such as airborne releases from deconstruction and natural evaporation from the lagoons, have become
the largest potential contributors to airborne dose, which was the case in 2024.

Breakdowns of the radioisotopes emitted from these stacks and PVUs in 2024 are shown in Tables C-1 to C-4 in Appendix C. The
concentrations of emitted radioisotopes were all below the DCSs. According to the real-time monitoring systems of on-site air,
on-site airborne radioactivity did not exceed the radiation exposure limits for site workers.

Ambient Air Monitoring Network (AAMN)
Since 2014, radioactivity samples for estimating off-site dose have been collected from 16 low-volume ambient air samplers
encircling the WVDP, covering 16 compass sectors. For the details of the AAMN program, please refer to “Off-Site Ambient Air
Monitoring Program” section in Chapter 4, “Environmental Monitoring.”

Airborne Dose Compliance Assessment
The airborne dose calculated from ambient air monitoring results is evaluated by comparing the measured radioactivity with
EPA standards using a sum-of-ratio analysis.

The 40 CFR 61, subpart H, Appendix E includes a tabulation of hypothetical radionuclide concentrations that would result in
a 10 mrem/year dose if a person were exposed to that concentration for a full year. A ratio (or %) of the average annual con-
centration of each isotope against the EPA standard for the isotope is calculated, and the ratios of all isotopes are summed by
compass sector. Compliance is demonstrated if the sum of these ratios
for all the isotopes measured at each sampling location is less than 1
(the annual 10 mrem dose standard.)

Table C-6 in Appendix C shows the results of this calculation in the col-
umn titled �Compliance Ratio.” The largest ratio observed in 2024 was
0.136 in the west-northwest direction. This result demonstrates that
the WVDP was in compliance with EPA regulations for airborne radio-
nuclide emissions in 2024.

5-6 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 Airborne Dose Estimates Using CAP88-PC at the WVDP

  History. The CAP88-PC model is widely used for evaluating doses and radiological risks when planning work activities
  that have the potential to release airborne radioactivity. This model estimates the human absorbed dose via ingestion,
  inhalation, air immersion, and ground surface pathways. The resulting dose is then used to demonstrate compliance with
  40 CFR 61, Subpart H (the NESHAP regulations). Version 4.1.1 of CAP88-PC (Trinity Engineering Associates, Inc., June 2021)
  is the most recent version approved by the EPA for demonstrating NESHAP compliance. However, any previous versions
  approved by the EPA can also be used for compliance.

  Previously, doses from airborne radioactive materials released from stacks and diffuse sources on the WVDP property
  were modeled using CAP88-PC to demonstrate compliance with the NESHAP. The off-site ambient air monitoring network
  (AAMN) was installed in 2012 in anticipation of future structure deconstruction work. In the following year, the dose
  estimate based on measurements at the AAMN samplers was compared against the dose estimate based on on-site
  sources to test the validity of the AAMN measurement method. The EPA reviewed both methods and granted the WVDP
  final approval to use the AAMN measurements to evaluate compliance with the 10 mrem NESHAP standard, as outlined in
  40 CFR 61, subpart H, Appendix E concentration values. A sum of ratios is calculated by dividing annual concentrations for
  each measured isotope by the Appendix E concentration values and summing these ratios for all the isotopes measured.
  Ratios of <1 demonstrate compliance with the NESHAP standard.

  Until September 2022, when the MPPB deconstruction began, most of the concentrations of radioisotopes measured at
  the AAMN samplers were nondetects (below the detection limits, or the uncertainty was larger than the result). To make
  conservative dose estimates from mostly nondetect results, the WVDP used the measurements (the larger of the result and
  its uncertainty) from the prevalent wind direction (NNW [north-northwest]) and applied CAP88 dose factors to them. For
  uncertainty associated with radiological analysis, refer to Chapter 8, “Radiation and Environmental Standards.”

  Since the start of MPPB deconstruction work, very low positive detections were observed on the AAMN samplers, and the
  dose modeling method was updated to estimate annual doses better. An overview of this updated process is described
  below.

  Individual dose calculation method. CAP88-PC was used to calculate a unit dose conversion factor (UDCF) at each
  AAMN location (compass direction and distance), using site-specific five-year average meteorological data. (See Chapter
  4, “Meteorological Monitoring” section.) With site-specific meteorological data, CAP88-PC calculates radiological
  concentration, dose, and dispersion (CHI/Q) factors given unit (1) input for each isotope at all AAMN locations. The
  calculated dose conversion factor is then used to compute the dose at each AAMN location based on the annual average
  concentration of each isotope at all locations. If positive detections are observed for more than one isotope at a given
  AAMN location, the resulting doses are summed to achieve the annual total dose at that AAMN location.

  Population dose calculation method. Because positive detections may be recorded at AAMN samplers in specific compass
  directions, the WVDP population dose calculation adopted direction-based methodology. Using the CAP88-PC dispersion
  factors, a site source-term in curies (Ci) can be determined for locations where positive detections were observed. With the
  source term established for a given isotope at the AAMN location, the population dose can be calculated for the directional
  sector through a 50-mile (80-km) radius. Population dose values are then summed for all sectors, where positive results
  were observed, and for all isotopes to produce the annual collective population dose out to a radius of 50 miles from the
  site.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 5-7

Dose from Waterborne Releases
Surface Water Standards. Currently, there are no waterborne dose standards set by the EPA or DOE for an individual
(MEOSI). DOE Order 458.1, “Radiation Protection of the Public and the Environment,” requires DOE facilities to limit annual
radiological exposure to the public to less than 100 mrem from all sources. For collective (population) dose, the DOE sets
100 person-rem as a guideline, or a threshold, that a facility needs to perform routine environmental pathway surveillance.

Drinking Water Standards. EPA standards also establish limits on the radiation dose to members of the public from liquid efflu-
ents through the National Primary Drinking Water Regulations (40 CFR Part 141). NYSDOH sets the limits for community water
supplies in the New York State Sanitary Code (10 NYCRR 5-1). The EPA and NYSDOH drinking water limit is 4 mrem/year.

For the WVDP’s dose estimate from drinking water is described in the following “Waterborne Dose Assessment Methodology”
section.

Waterborne Dose Assessment Methodology

Potential doses to near-site residents and the local population from the waterborne pathway are estimated using software,
GENII v2, a DOE-approved dose modeling software. The WVDP uses a chronic surface exposure model provided by GENII. The
dose from the WVDP activities through the water pathway to a hypothetical MEOSI is estimated at Felton Bridge, the first point
of public access to Cattaraugus Creek downstream of the WVDP. For the waterborne collective dose, the total dose is estimated
for the population within a 50-mile (80-km) radius from the WVDP. GENII incorporates multiple exposure pathways into its radi-
ation exposure models, including food (meat, fish, vegetables, fruits, grains, and eggs), milk consumption, exposure from water
sports activities, and exposure from soil. Although the individual waterborne model does not include drinking water ingestion
from Cattaraugus Creek water, the collective dose model incorporates drinking water ingestion from Lake Erie. Lake Erie is the
major drinking water source for the residents of western New York.

In waterborne dose modeling, annual averages of radioisotope concentrations at three on-site discharge points are used as
source terms (source concentrations.) One is the SPDES outfall 001 (sampling location WNSP001 on lagoon 3), which is a con-
trolled discharge point. The other two discharge points are the northeast swamp drainage (sampled at WNSWAMP) and north
swamp drainage (sampled at WNSW74A); both are natural drainages.

According to the model results, the most important waterborne exposure pathway for a nearby individual is the consumption
of fish from Cattaraugus Creek, followed by the consumption of vegetables and milk. The major source of waterborne exposure
for the population is drinking water, followed by the consumption of milk and fish.

2024 Potential Maximum Waterborne Dose to an Off-Site Individual
Controlled discharges with low levels of radioactivity from SPDES outfall 001 (lagoon 3 discharges) and natural discharge from
WNSW74A and WNSWAMP continued in 2024. The concentrations of discharged isotopes and flow volumes from these dis-
charges are reported in Tables B-2A, B-4A ,and B-4B. The radioactivity of the discharged isotopes was multiplied by the Unit
Dose Factors (UDFs) to calculate the dose to the MEOSI and the dose to the population. Additional details about the surface
waterborne dose modeling and the UDFs used in the modeling are described in the inset box, “Using Dose Conversion Factors
to Estimate Waterborne Dose” on page 5-10.

In 2024, a hypothetical MEOSI could have received a maximum waterborne dose of 0.004 mrem (0.00004 mSv) from the iso-
topes discharged from those three discharge points, a 57% reduction compared with 2023. The annual dose from the radio-
activity in controlled discharges from the outfall 001 was 0.0013 mrem (0.000013 mSv,) and the annual dose from other two
natural drainages was 0.0026 mrem (0.000026 mSv.) In 2024, the total curies discharged from outfall 001 increased compared
with 2023; however, the total curies discharged from WNSWAMP and WNSW74A decreased, leading to a 30% decrease in the
total curies with all isotopes combined.

5-8 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

Strontium-90 contributed approximately 57% of the total dose generated from the outfall 001 discharges in 2024, followed by
cesium-137, which contributed 27% of the total dose. Most of the dose from those natural drainages was attributable to stron-
tium-90 from the WNSWAMP (82% of the total dose from WNSWAMP) and WNSW74A (24% of the total dose from WNSW74A)
and cesium-137 from WNSW74A (3% of the total dose from WNSW74A).

Distributions of radioisotope contributions in the waterborne dose in 2024 are summarized in Figure 5-4. As in previous years,
strontium-90 and cesium-137 were the two major sources of waterborne dose. Compared with the distributions in previous
years, the proportion of ‘All others’ increased in 2024. Among ‘All others,’ the isotope that showed the largest percent increase
in 2024 was carbon-14 (an annual total of 9.13E-04 mrem and about 23% of the total waterborne dose). The increases are
attributed to the GENII model setting update described in the inset, “Using Dose Conversion Factors to Estimate Waterborne
Dose” on page 5-10. Carbon-14 emits low-energy beta radiation and is produced naturally in the atmosphere and during nuclear
energy generation and nuclear weapons tests. The annual mean concentrations of carbon-14 at the three discharge points were
about four orders of magnitude smaller than the DCS concentration.

2024 Collective Population Dose (Waterborne)
The collective dose to the population living within a 50-mile (80-km) radius of the WVDP was 0.038 person-rem (0.00038
person-Sv) in 2024. This is a very small fraction of the DOE’s annual 100 person-rem dose guidance. The waterborne collective
dose decreased by 16% compared with the collective dose in 2023.

Figure 5-5 on page 5-12 illustrates the model-estimated dose from the water pathway alone over the past 20 years. The esti-
mated waterborne dose has decreased due to the reduction of strontium-90 concentrations in the WNSWAMP after the PTW
installation in 2010, as well as the water management update, which reduced the volume of industrial water flowing into lagoon
3 (and discharged from outfall 001).

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 5-9

 Using Dose Conversion Factors To Estimate Waterborne Dose
 The environmental dosimetry computer code, GENII version 2.10.2b, is used to calculate site-specific Unit Dose Factors
 (UDFs) for waterborne radioactivity releases and dispersion of these effluents from the WVDP. The annual waterborne dose
 from the WVDP activities is estimated by multiplying the radioactivity in curies discharged from on-site discharge points by
 their respective isotope UDFs, and summing the dose contribution from each isotope.

 The annual individual (MEOSI) dose and collective (population) dose were calculated using the site-specific parameters as
 well as GENII recommended parameters. Those parameters include, but are not limited to: local food consumption data,
 local farming/food production data, water sports activities in the area, soil characteristics, and climate data. The MEOSI
 dose modeling point is at Felton Bridge on Cattaraugus Creek, the first public access point to the creek water downstream
 of the WVDP site. The collective dose was estimated at the Main Bridge on Cattaraugus Creek in Gowanda, NY, for the popu-
 lation (approximately 1.6 million) within a 50-mile (80-km) radius of the WVDP. The MEOSI model does not include drinking
 water consumption because water from Cattataugus Creek is not used for public potable water. The collective dose model
 includes drinking water consumption because water in Lake Erie is the major source of drinking water for the residents in
 Western New York.

 The annual average concentrations of three isotopes at three on-site discharge points (outfall 001 [WNS001], WNSAMP, and
 WNS74A) are used to calculate radioactivity in curies discharged during the year.

 The liquid effluents from those discharge points travel to Erdman Brook and Franks Creek on site, then leave the WVDP
 premises and flow into Buttermilk Creek east of the site. Buttermilk Creek merges into Cattaraugus Creek north of the
 WVDP, and then Cattaraugus Creek discharges into Lake Erie. Felton Bridge on Cattaraugus Creek is approximately three
 miles (4.8 km) from the WVDP; the Main Bridge in Gowanda on Cattaraugus Creek is approximately 12 miles (19 km), Lake
 Erie is about 28 miles (45 km) downstream. For dose calculations, dilution factors for interim streams between the WVDP
 and Cattaraugus Creek as well as the dilution factor in Lake Erie are not considered. Thus the waterborne dose modeling
 results are considered to be conservative.

 At the WVDP, GENII version 1 was used for waterborne dose modeling through 2023. In 2024, the software version was
 updated to version 2, along with the aforementioned input values, as well as the population for the collective dose calcula-
 tion. GENII’s internal conversion factors were updated to match DOE DCS 2022 water ingestion effective dose coefficients.
 When the actual data is not available, recommended values are used (PNNL 2013).

 Due to these updates, the dose conversion factors per unit curie (Ci) inputs were adjusted, resulting in reduced MEOSI and
 collective dose reductions that exceed the actual reductions of the source curies described in the body text and presented
 in Figure 5-5.

5-10 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

Dose From Air and Water Pathways
The total estimated potential dose from the WVDP in 2024 is a combination of a dose from airborne exposure based on mea-
surements from the AAMN and a modeled dose from water exposure.

2024 Total Dose (Air and Water)
Table 5-2 summarizes the dose from both the air and water exposure pathways. The potential individual dose to the public from
both airborne and liquid effluents released from the WVDP activities in 2024 was 0.184 mrem (0.00184 mSv), 0.18 mrem from
the air pathway, plus 0.004 mrem from the water pathway. This dose is 0.18% of the 100-mrem (1-mSv) annual limit in DOE
Order 458.1.

Table 5-3 on page 5-13 presents the total curies (and in Becquerels) by radionuclide types released to air and water from all
sources at the WVDP. The air dose total is computed from measured air concentrations at the on-site stacks and from estimated
diffuse sources such as the evaporation from the lagoons and facility deconstruction. The water total is computed from mea-
sured concentrations of controlled surface water discharges and natural drainage. Excluding radon (Rn-220), Table 5-3 shows
that in 2024, the total curies released to surface water were greater than the total curies released to the air.

Dose from Food Samples
The dose from local food consumption is estimated based on actual food samples collected near the WVDP. Vegetables, fruit,
milk, venison, and fish samples from the WVDP vicinity are collected and analyzed for radiological constituents. (Biological
sampling locations are shown on Figures A-11 and A-14.) As stated in Chapter 4, “Environmental Monitoring,” milk and
venison (deer meat) samples are collected every year and fish, apples, beans, and corn are collected every five years. The five-
year interval samples were last sampled in 2022. The DOE has developed Ingestion Dose Conversion Factors (DCFs) for DOE
sites to convert measured radioactivity concentrations in food into dose. The ingested radioactivity in food multiplied by these
DCFs provides the estimated maximum potential dose from the food only pathway. (Note: The estimated dose from the food
pathway for 2024 uses the DCFs from DOE/EH-0071. The DCFs for the radioisotopes used in the food dose provided in DOE/
EH-0071 are very similar to, and more conservative than the more recent DCFs for these isotopes for ingested water and milk
in DOE-STD-1196-2022.)

2024 Estimated Dose from Food

The estimated ingestion dose from food items, including 2024 samples of milk and venison and 2022 samples of fish, fruit,
and crops, was 0.4 mrem/year for an individual residing in the regions of the WVDP. It was 0.1% of natural radiation that a U.S.
resident would receive annually (310 mrem/year). Although strontium-90 and cesium-137 were positively detected in venison
samples, those concentrations were statistically indistinguishable from the concentrations in the background samples. (See
“2024 Food Monitoring Update” section in Chapter 4, for more details.)

Dose to Biota
Radionuclides from both natural and artificial sources may be found in environmental media such as water, sediments, and soils.
Radiological controls sufficient to protect humans may not adequately protect other living organisms because plant and animal
populations residing in or near these media or taking food or water from these media may be exposed to a greater extent than
are humans.

DOE Order 458.1 requires protection of the local biota from potential adverse effects from radioactivity released from DOE
site operations and has established dose rate limits to assist in evaluating the effects. A description of the biota dose standard
is provided in the inset box, “Biota Dose Modeling Methodology using RESRAD-BIOTA” on page 5-15. The technical standard,
DOE-STD-1153-2002, “A Graded Approach for Evaluating Radiation Doses to Aquatic and Terrestrial Biota” was streamlined and
reissued in early 2019 as DOE-STD-1153-2019, of the same title.

The RESRAD-BIOTA model uses Biota Concentration Guides (BCGs) to convert measured concentrations in environmental media
to dose to the biota. BCGs are defined as the maximum concentration of a radionuclide in soil, sediment, or water that would not
result in an exceedance of the protective dose limit for aquatic and terrestrial biota. The methodology for estimating dose to biota
involves comparing measured concentrations in the environment with modeled concentrations at which known dose effects have
been predicted for specific plants and animals. The model approach is termed ‘graded’ (or ‘tiered’) because it applies multiple
levels of screening to provide refinements and additional capabilities desired by a broad range of model users (Yu et al., 2003).
The model outputs annual doses to aquatic biota (aquatic animal and riparian animal) and terrestrial biota (terrestrial plant and
terrestrial animal).

Calculated doses in each level of screening are assessed with the DOE standard presented in DOE-STD-1153-2019, Table 1-1,
for compliance:
 <1.0 rad/d for aquatic animals;
 <0.1 rad/d for riparian animals;
 <1.0 rad/d for terrestrial plants; and
 <0.1 rad/d for terrestrial animals.

 (Note that the absorbed dose unit [rad] is used for biota instead of the units used for indicating human risk [rem]).

2024 Biota Dose Modeling Results
The RESRAD-BIOTA model was run using WVDP site-specific input concentrations of surface water, soil, and sediment to assess
radiation exposure to biota on the WVDP site. For 2024, the surface water data measured in 2024 and soil and sediment data
up to 2022 were used in the model. (Soil and sediment samples are collected every five years.)

Following the graded approach, the maximum potential biota dose was first modeled using the maximum of measured radionu-
clide concentrations from surface waters, sediments, and soils. The resulting dose exceeded applicable limits for both aquatic
and terrestrial evaluations in 2024.

Then, the biota dose model was run again using estimates of average radionuclide concentrations derived from measurements
from the same medium used for the maximum biota dose assessment. Average concentrations more closely represent actual
environmental conditions. The resulting doses using average concentrations were below limits and are summarized in Table
5-4. The measurements of 12 radioisotopes are input in the RESRAD-BIOTA model. However, only the results for cesium-137
and strontium-90 are shown in Table 5-4 because these two isotopes contribute the largest components of both aquatic and
terrestrial doses to biota, based on air and water monitoring data at the WVDP.

Table 5-4 shows that the sums of fractions for the aquatic and terrestrial evaluations were 2.43E-01 and 5.71E-01, respectively,
compared with the BCGs, with the higher dose occurring in the terrestrial system. The sum of fractions for both the aquatic and
terrestrial evaluations was less than 1.0, indicating that applicable BCGs were not exceeded; therefore populations of aquatic
and terrestrial biota (both plants and animals) on the WNYNSC premises are not being exposed to doses above DOE standards.

5-14 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 Biota Dose Modeling Methodology Using RESRAD-BIOTA
  The DOE has prepared a technical standard that provides methods and guidance to be used to evaluate doses of
  ionizing radiation to populations of aquatic animals, riparian animals, terrestrial plants, and terrestrial animals. Methods
  in this technical standard, “A Graded Approach for Evaluating Radiation Doses to Aquatic and Terrestrial Biota” (DOE-
  STD-1153-2019, February 2019), are used to evaluate radiation doses to aquatic and terrestrial biota within the confines of
  the WNYNSC, which includes the WVDP.

  RESRAD-BIOTA (version 1.8, April 2016), a calculation tool provided by the DOE for implementing the technical standard,
  is used to compare existing radionuclide concentration data from environmental sampling with Biota Concentration Guide
  (BCG) screening values and to estimate upper bounding doses to biota.

  Average and maximum concentration data of soil, sediment, and surface water concentrations from the target area are
  input into RESRAD-BIOTA to perform the graded surveillance of dose to biota. At the WVDP, surface water data obtained
  from the current year and soil and sediment data from multiple years are used for modeling. Surface water is sampled
  with the frequencies determined by the WVDP sampling program for each location. The soil and sediment sampling fre-
  quency was changed from annually to every five years in 2007. Therefore, for 2024, the most recent sediment samples
  included samples collected from 2005–2007, 2012, 2017 and 2022 and the most recent routine on-site surface soil sam-
  pling includes samples collected from 1995–2007, 2012, 2017 and 2022. Historical on-site surface soil sampling data from
  several special projects were also used. A longer sampling interval is used for soil and sediment because radionuclide
  concentrations change more rapidly over time in surface waters than in sediments and soils.

  The concentration for each radionuclide in each medium is divided by its corresponding BCG to calculate a partial fraction
  for each nuclide in each medium. Partial fractions for each medium were added to produce a sum of fractions. Exposures
  from the aquatic pathway may be assumed to be less than the aquatic dose limit from DOE-STD-1153-2019 if the sum of
  fractions for the water medium plus that for the sediment medium is less than 1.0. Similarly, exposures from the terrestrial
  pathway may be assumed to be less than the proposed dose limits for both terrestrial plants and animals if the sum of
  fractions for the water medium plus that for the soil medium is less than 1.0.

The populations of organisms most sensitive (most likely adversely affected) to strontium-90 and cesium-137 via the aquatic
and terrestrial pathways were riparian animals (such as the raccoon [aquatic dose]) and terrestrial animals (such as the wood-
chuck [terrestrial dose]). Populations of both animals are found on the WNYNSC.

Release of Materials Containing Residual Radioactivity

In addition to discharges to the environment, the release of property containing residual radioactive materials is considered a
potential contributor to the dose received by the public, as set forth in DOE Order 458.1.

The DOE sites are authorized to release and clear scrap metals, including materials possibly contaminated with radioactivity.
However, the release procedure has to adhere to the requirements outlined in DOE Order 458.1, “Radiation Protection of
the Public and the Environment.” Programs to ensure the safe release of materials from the DOE sites have been established,
including radiological assessment and survey procedures. A release of scrap metals is permitted only when it is assured that the
release does not incur adverse effects on either the public or the environment. No potentially radiologically contaminated scrap
metals were released from the WVDP in 2024.

Risk Assessment
Very high doses of radiation are known to cause adverse health effects, including cancer in humans and other organisms. For
example, 50 rem (50,000 mrem) of radiation exposure causes damage to blood cells. 50,000 mrem is about five million times
the radiation a patient is exposed to for a chest x-ray (0.01 rem) (The U.S. CDC [U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention],

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 5-15

2024). Over the past several decades, the radiation health physics community has conducted considerable research into the
biological effects of a prolonged exposure to low-dose radiation to develop risk estimates for cancer and other health effects.
‘low-dose radiation’ refers to radiation levels of near zero to 10 rem (10,000 mrem) or dose rates of less than 500 mrem per
hour (National Academies of Science, Engineering, and Medicine, 2022).

The BEIR VII Phase 2 report, “Health Risks from Exposure to Low Levels of Ionizing Radiation” (NRC, 2006) estimated that,
assuming a sex and age distribution similar to that of the entire U.S. population, on average, approximately 1 person in 100
would be expected to develop cancer from a radiation dose of 10,000 mrem (100 mSv), while approximately 42 of the 100
individuals would be expected to develop cancer from all other causes. The Interagency Steering Committee on Radiation
Standards of the EPA (ISCORS, 2002) and DOE guidance estimate that the probability of fatal cancer occurring from exposure to
radioactivity is between one and six cancer cases per 10,000 people who are each exposed to one rem (1,000 mrem [10 mSv])
(i.e., a risk coefficient of between 0.0001 and 0.0006). These assumptions are often based on a cancer risk model known as the
linear no-threshold (LNT) model.

Discussions about and a validation study of the LNT model are ongoing (Trabalka et al., 2017). Some scientists argue that the
cancer risk is not linearly correlated to exposure to low levels of radiation, citing human cells’ natural ability to cure minor dam-
age, or citing the beneficial effect, homesis, on organisms of low-dose radiation (Agathokleous, 2024). Some research suggests
that researchers should consider various cancer triggers (or causes), including historical data, to estimate cancer risk from radi-
ation exposure (Bencko, 2011, Kellerer, 2000).

The radiological effluents from the WVDP are very low in radioactivity in all media. The predicted dose from all pathways
in 2024 from the WVDP activities was 0.184 mrem or 0.00184 mSv. Employing the ISCORS’ probability of fatal cancer from
radiation of six persons per 10,000 people per one rem, a cancer risk from the WVDP activities in 2024 was 11 cases per 100
million people. Figure 5 -1, earlier in this chapter, provides a more generic perspective on the small level of the emissions from
the WVDP activities.

Dose Assessment Summary
The 2024 estimated dose (0.184 mrem [0.00184 mSv]) from the WVDP activities to an off-site resident is far below the federal
standard of 100 mrem for dose from all pathways allowed from any DOE site operations in a calendar year, confirming that
efforts at the WVDP to minimize radiological releases are consistent with the ALARA philosophy of radiation protection.

The collective population dose was also assessed and found to be orders of magnitude below the natural background radiation
dose. The estimated risk to an individual residing near the WVDP from airborne and waterborne releases is well below the
range considered by the ICRP to be a reasonable risk for any member of the public. Radioactivity concentrations in milk and
venison samples were not distinguishable from those of background. Biota dose estimates indicated that populations of plants
and animals at the WVDP are only exposed to a fraction of the DOE standards for dose to biota.

Based on the overall dose assessment, the WVDP was found to be in compliance with applicable effluent radiological guidelines
and standards during 2024.

5-18 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

 CHAPTER 6
 GROUNDWATER PROTECTION PROGRAM

 • Overall program summary. Groundwater Monitoring Program (GMP) activities at the WVDP were performed in compli-
 ance with requirements throughout the calendar year 2024.

 • PTW performance. The Permeable Treatment Wall (PTW) installed in 2010 continued to remove strontium-90 from the
 groundwater plume as the groundwater passes through the wall.

 • North Plateau Plume. The continued use of a sand/salt deicing material during the 2023-2024 winter resulted in lower,
 stabilized strontium-90 concentration levels in the north plateau plume.

 • Other Areas of North Plateau. Downgradient of the Construction and Demolition Debris Landfill (CDDL), concentrations of
 VOCs have decreased to nondetectable levels.

 • South Plateau and NDA. The interim measures installed at the NDA on the south plateau continue to work effectively. The
 geomembrane cover and upgradient slurry wall have minimized the infiltration and migration of water into the NDA and
 have significantly reduced the volume of water collecting in the interceptor trench.

 • Emerging Contaminants of Concern. Samples for analysis of per- and polyfluoroalkyl (PFAS) substances and emerging
 chemicals were collected from the drinking water supply well in 2024, and the results were nondetect.

 • Next Decommissioning Phase. Plans for activities during the next phase (Phase 1B) of WVDP decommissioning are being
 shared with stakeholders.

WVDP Groundwater Monitoring Program Overview
The WVDP groundwater monitoring program (WVDP GMP) consists of three areas of focus, based on compliance requirements,
specific physical locations, and monitoring purposes.

 1. Groundwater Monitoring Program (GMP) – Site-wide monitoring/environmental surveillance per DOE Orders and RCRA
 §3008(h) Compliance using wells located across the north and south plateaus.

 2. Permeable Treatment Wall Performance Monitoring Program (PTWPMP) – Monitoring of the PTW using dedicated wells,
 which are located adjacent to and within the PTW.

 3. North Plateau Groundwater Monitoring Program (NPGMP) – Supplements the PTWPMP using additional wells located
 further upgradient and downgradient of the PTW.

This chapter outlines the program details and presents the monitoring results for calendar year 2024. Monitoring results for the
PTWPMP and NPGMP are discussed together.

Geology and Hydrogeology at the WVDP
The WNYNSC is situated upon a layered sequence of glacial sediments that fill a steep-sided bedrock valley composed of
interbedded shales and siltstones (Rickard, 1975). The glacial sediments overlying the bedrock consist of three silt- and clay-rich
glacial tills of Lavery, Kent, and possibly Olean deposits, which are separated by stratified fluvio-lacustrine deposits (silty or silty/
sandy lakebed sediments). (See Table 6-1 for a detailed description of each geologic layer.)

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 6-1

Listed in progression from bottom to top, the bedrock beneath the WVDP is overlain by the Kent till (KT), followed by the Kent
Recessional Sequence (KRS) (layers of clay or silty clay topped by coarser sands and gravels), and the unweathered Lavery Till
(ULT). See Figure 6-1 and Table 6-1 for the geographic distribution and additional description of these units.

Erdman Brook traverses the WVDP site, dividing it into a north and south plateau. The uppermost surficial sediments covering
the north and south plateaus are quite different. On the south plateau, the ULT is exposed at the surface, where it is weathered
and fractured, and is referred to as the weathered Lavery Till (WLT). On the north plateau, the ULT is covered by an alluvial sand
and gravel (S&G) unit, composed of silty sands and gravels. It can be further divided into two subunits: the thick-bedded unit
(TBU), a thick bed of silty sands and gravels, and the slackwater sequence (SWS), composed of thin layered silts and fine sands.
Within the ULT on the north plateau is a smaller unit called the Lavery Till sand (LTS), an isolated sandy unit entirely contained
within the till (WVNSCO, January 1993; WVNSCO, February 1993).

The MPPB, WTF, and lagoons are located on the north plateau. The drum cell, NDA, and SDA are located on the south plateau.
The ULT and Kent till have relatively low permeability, and groundwater from the S&G and WLT must flow through the ULT to
reach the KRS and deeper underlying geologic units. Therefore, the ULT, KRS, Kent till, and bedrock do not provide predominant
pathways for contaminant movement from the WVDP and are not discussed here. The S&G unit and the WLT are generally re-
garded as the predominant routes for contaminant migration from the WVDP via groundwater.

The majority of groundwater migrating across the site discharges from the S&G and WLT into the surrounding streams, which
eventually flow into Cattaraugus Creek and then into Lake Erie. Past surveys determined that no community public water sup-
plies are drawn from groundwater downgradient of the site or from Cattaraugus Creek downstream of the WVDP. Upgradient
of the site, groundwater is used as a public and private drinking water supply by local residents.

Site Groundwater Use
Since 2014, two bedrock water supply wells, WNDWELL 1 and WNDWELL 2, have been used to provide the site’s primary water
supply. These two wells are located upgradient of the site facilities and areas of contamination. (See Figure A-8.) Chemical and
radiological sampling is performed routinely, and sampling results for drinking water quality parameters are provided to the
Cattaraugus County Health Department (CCHD). Site groundwater in the shallow, unconsolidated glacial sediments located
above the bedrock is not used for drinking or operational purposes, nor is WVDP effluent discharged directly to groundwater.
Drinking water data are presented in Appendix B-5.

Groundwater Monitoring Program (GMP)
The WVDP GMP was designed to comply with DOE Order 458.1, Section 4.i.2, which states that “Groundwater must be protect-
ed from radiological contamination to ensure compliance with dose limits in the Order and consistent with ALARA process re-
quirements. DOE sites must ensure that: baseline conditions of the groundwater quantity and quality are documented; possible
sources of, and potential for, radiological contamination are identified and assessed; strategies to control radiological contam-
ination are documented and implemented; monitoring methodologies are documented and implemented; and groundwater
monitoring activities are integrated with other environmental monitoring activities.”

The GMP also complies with the requirements of RCRA §3008(h) Administrative Order on Consent and the conclusions in the
RFI reports, which require routine monitoring of certain analytes at specified groundwater monitoring locations.

Groundwater conditions are monitored in the subsurface geologic units that represent potential routes of contaminant migra-
tion. The monitoring analytes and frequency are tailored for each well, depending upon location, nearby historical site opera-
tions, and the potential for contaminant migration.

Results are evaluated to identify current conditions and historical trends, and final conclusions are reported to both state and fed-
eral agencies in addition to the discussions in this report. Methods for evaluating groundwater data are described in Appendix D.
Highlights of the site groundwater monitoring history and the evolution of the GMP are summarized at the end of this chapter
in Table 6-6.

Routine Groundwater Monitoring
Groundwater Monitoring Network

Groundwater is monitored across the north and south plateaus using wells installed in the first six of the seven geologic units
described in Table 6-1. In 2024, GMP samples were collected from 64 on-site, routine groundwater monitoring locations, which
included:

  • 58 monitoring wells located across the north and south plateaus;

  • five groundwater seepage points, located along the northeast edge of the north plateau, where groundwater from the
 S&G unit discharges to the ground surface along the banks of Franks Creek; and

  • the trench sump (NDATR) located within the interceptor trench installed at the NDA .

Well locations are displayed on Figures A-9 and A-10 in Appendix A. Many of the wells are also used for monitoring releases
from one or more of the SWMUs or Super SWMUs (SSWMUs) on site (See Table 6-2) per the RCRA §3008(h) Consent Order

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024 6-5

and the conclusions in the RFI reports requiring routine monitoring of certain analytes at specified groundwater monitoring
locations. For RCRA discussions, refer to Chapter 2, “Environmental Compliance Summary” and the groundwater schedule in
Table A-2. No wells were installed or decommissioned during 2024.

Groundwater Elevation Monitoring

Groundwater elevations are measured in the monitoring network wells in conjunction with the quarterly analytical sampling.
These data are used to map groundwater flow directions. Long-term trend graphs are used to evaluate variations in ground-
water elevations over time, which may result from cyclical seasonal fluctuations or on-site operations such as installing water
diversions (e.g., geomembrane covers, trenches, or slurry walls), and the groundwater treatment systems (e.g., the full-scale
PTW).

Groundwater elevation mapping of the WLT on the south plateau helps evaluate the effectiveness of the NDA interceptor
trench, the slurry wall, and geomembrane cover. (See “South Plateau and NDA Groundwater Monitoring” in this chapter.)

North Plateau Strontium-90 Plume
The groundwater well sampling network on the north plateau monitors potential and existing sources of chemical and radiologi-
cal contaminants. This includes areas of previously detected contamination such as the CDDL and the former lagoon 1 area. The
current focus of radiological groundwater monitoring on the north plateau is the gross beta groundwater plume.

Routine groundwater monitoring in the north plateau S&G unit is provided by 36 monitoring wells and five groundwater seep-
age locations. Elevated gross beta activity has been detected in groundwater from the S&G unit, the shallowest geologic unit
on the north plateau, since 1993. Historical monitoring of groundwater has established that strontium-90 is the predominant
beta emitter found in the groundwater plume. Strontium-90 accounts for approximately one-half of the gross beta activity be-
cause the gross beta activity includes both strontium-90 and its daughter product, yttrium-90. Therefore, monitoring wells are
routinely sampled only for gross beta concentrations, supported by periodic sampling at select wells for strontium-90 analysis.

For the discussions about gross beta and strontium-90 in this report, the strontium-90 DCS is used as a comparison level for
both gross beta and strontium-90 concentrations. (See Chapter 8, “Radiation and Environmental Standards” for a discussion of
the DOE Derived Concentration Standards [DCSs], and Table 8-4 for a list of the DCSs for radionuclides of interest at the WVDP.)

Updates for 2024 GMP
Historical Extents of Groundwater Plume

Figure 6-2 on page 6-8 illustrates the extent of the groundwater plume in the S&G unit, as defined by the 1.0E-06 µCi/mL gross
beta isopleth, at three time intervals (1994, 2010, and 2024). Both the NYSDEC TOGS.1.1.1 Class GA Groundwater Quality Stan-
dard, and the former DOE Derived Concentration Guide (DCG) for strontium-90 are set to 1.0E-06 µCi/mL. The WVDP Ground-
water Screening Level (GSL) for strontium-90 is also based on the NYSDEC TOGS Standard (Table D-1A). The 1994 isopleth shows
the results of the subsurface characterization program that measured gross beta concentrations to define the outline of the
plume discovered in 1993. The plume originated from beneath the MPPB. (See the “1994” row in Table 6-6). The 2010 isopleth
shows the furthest extent of the plume’s northeastward migration before the installation of the PTW in 2010. The 2024 isopleth
shows the current extent of the plume at the 1.0E-06 µCi/mL level.

As shown in Figure 6-2, while the plume’s western boundary has not changed significantly since 1994, the plume has continued
to migrate to the northeast and east across the north plateau, extending its spatial extent toward those directions. The leading
edge of the plume divided into three narrow lobes as a result of the uneven distribution of coarse and fine soils within the S&G
unit, which creates preferential pathways for groundwater flow.

A comparison of the 1.0E-06 µCi/mL gross beta isopleths for 2010 and 2024 shows that the 2024 isopleths in the western and
central lobes have elongated as a result of the northeastward flow of groundwater across the north plateau. In general, the por-
tion of the plume shown downgradient of the PTW is due to residual gross beta activity from groundwater that had previously
migrated past the location of the PTW prior to its installation and has been partially attenuated and migrated off-site. Some

6-6 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2024

gross beta activity in groundwater migrating into the PTW has intermittently begun to exit the PTW in certain areas over the
past few years, resulting in increased activity at a few locations on the downgradient side.

On Figure 6-2, flow arrows show groundwater flow directions across the north plateau. Groundwater generally flows northeast
across most of the north plateau; however, in the area directly south of the central portion of the PTW, groundwater flows
eastward. Some of the groundwater entering the PTW flows eastward within the PTW, diverting upgradient groundwater to the
east. This eastward flow has facilitated the migration of the groundwater plume to the eastern edge of the plateau.

Gross beta concentration trends over the last 10 years at selected monitoring wells located within the plume are shown on
Figures 6-4 through Figures 6-8. These data are plotted on a log scale; therefore, an increase from one gridline to the next
represents a 10-fold increase in concentration. The log scale was used to enable comparison between data from background
locations (with concentrations in the 1.0E-09 µCi/mL range) and data from the central plume (with concentrations in the 1.0E-04
µCi/mL range, 100,000 times higher than the background).

2024 Groundwater Plume Concentrations

Figure 6-3 shows the current, complete north plateau groundwater plume based upon sampling results from December 2024.
Each colored area represents a 10-fold increase in gross beta concentration, ranging from 1.0E-08 to 1.0E-04 µCi/mL. The high-
est concentration range (≥1.0E-04 µCi/mL) is the area that originates beneath the former MPPB and extends to the northeast
towards the PTW, where it begins to divide into narrow lobes, presumably flowing along preferential pathways within the
subsurface sediments. The next highest range includes concentrations between 1.0E-4 and 1.0E-05 µCi/mL; this range extends
from the source area up to the PTW, where its further migration is mostly halted by being treated within the zeolite of the PTW.

As mentioned earlier, some gross beta activity intermittently is able to migrate through the PTW and exit from the downgradi-
ent side in areas where the zeolite is becoming saturated with strontium-90 and other positively charged ions.

2024 Monitoring Results of the Plume Close to the Source

Figures 6-4 through 6-6 illustrate the annual average gross beta concentrations in wells monitoring the groundwater plume in
the main portion of the north plateau, from the source area up to the PTW. The background concentrations (orange lines) on
these graphs represent the averages of monitoring results at wells 301, 401, 706, and 1302, which are located upgradient (on
the west side) of the MPPB. Figure 6-4 illustrates the annual average gross beta concentrations in groundwater wells located
immediately downgradient of the MPPB and along the western edge of the plume (at well 8609). Well 408 and the four MPPB
wells (MP-01, -02, -03, and -04), located northeast of the MPPB closest to the source area, historically exhibit the highest gross
beta concentrations of any routinely monitored wells in the GMP. MP-01 and -02 have not been sampled since MPPB decon-
struction started. The gross beta concentrations decreased moderately between 2015 and 2019, then increased from 2019
through 2021. Beginning in 2021, strontium-90 concentrations, which counts for about half of gross beta, observed in the wells

2024 Monitoring Results of Downgradient of the PTW

Figure 6-7 on the previous page illustrates gross beta concentrations at monitoring wells 801, 803, 804, and 8612, downgradient
of the PTW. In 2024, Gross beta concentrations increased at wells 803 and 8612 as compared to 2023. Wells 803 and 8612 are in the
central lobe of the plume where gross beta activity had migrated past the PTW before it was installed in 2010, as indicated on Figure
6-2. These increasing trends have been observed since 2020. Gross beta concentrations at well 8612 exceeded the strontium-90 DCS
(1.7E-06 µCi/mL) for the first time in 2023. An investigation to evaluate options for improving the PTW effectiveness and increas-
ing its longevity was completed in 2024, and potential further actions are being evaluated. (For further discussion of the PTW
performance evaluation, refer to “Permeable Treatment Wall Performance Monitoring Program (PTWPMP)” on page 6-18. ) At
well 801, immediately downgradient of the PTW, the figure suggests that gross beta concentrations began to decrease through
2024.

111 and 8605 are above the strontium-90 DCS. Over the last ten years, the average gross beta concentrations have remained

Tritium in North Plateau Groundwater

Tritium, a fission product of the nuclear power fuel cycle, has historically been detected on the north plateau. Elevated tritium
concentrations have been observed downgradient of the MPPB and adjacent to and downgradient of the lagoon system. The
tritium activities are attributed to residual tritium in the nuclear fuel that was reprocessed on the site by NFS.

As shown in Table 6-3, the maximum tritium concentration measured in groundwater from the north plateau in 2024,
3.72E-07 µCi/mL, occurred at well MP-03, located on the east side of the MPPB and northeast of the former lagoon 1. The
concentration was four orders of magnitude below the DOE DCS. (See Figure 6-2 for the well location) and was lower than the
maximum concentration in 2023. Overall, the maximum tritium concentrations among wells have decreased over the years.

Radioisotopic Sampling Results on the North Plateau

Several groundwater monitoring wells in the north plateau S&G unit were analyzed for a select list of radionuclides in 2024,
in addition to gross alpha, gross beta, tritium, and strontium-90. (See Table A-2 for sampling program details.) The maximum
concentrations of radionuclides, other than tritium, were detected at either 104, 401, MP-03, or MP-04 on the north plateau.
Plutonium-239/240 were not detected in 2024; however, iodine-129 was detected at MP-04 with the maximum concentration
of 2.42E-08 µCi/mL, which is above the GSL but below the DCS value (5.7E-07 µCi/mL). The presumed source areas, from which
low levels of iodine-129 periodically migrate, will be removed under the EIS Phase 1B soil remediation activities.

“South Plateau and NDA Groundwater Monitoring,” later in this chapter, provides discussion of sampling results on the south
plateau.

Groundwater Monitoring Downgradient of the Waste Tank Farm (WTF)

Liquid radioactive waste remaining in the underground HLW tanks after the completion of the vitrification process is being
stabilized by the T&VDS, which reduces the liquid volume in the tanks and vaults through evaporation. Three of the tanks have
become dry, and the liquid levels in the fourth tank are decreasing. (See Chapter 2, “Environmental Compliance Summary” for
additional information.)

Throughout and after waste processing activities at the WTF, groundwater controls have been implemented by periodic pump-
ing of a dewatering well located adjacent to the tank vaults. Pumping of the well reduces the upward hydrostatic pressure on
the tanks, and maintains an inward hydraulic gradient toward the tanks, thereby inhibiting migration of potential leaks from
the tanks.

At groundwater monitoring well 8607 near the WTF, elevated gross beta concentrations have been observed since 1994. (See
the location of well 8607 on Figure 6-3.) The concentrations have oscillated between below 1.00E-08 and 5.00E-08 except for a
few short-term concentration spikes due to the activities at the WTF area. The maximum gross beta concentration at well 8607
in 2024 was 3.07E-08 µCi/mL in June.
